Alexandre Ghiti

1 items
Patches Submitted (1)
Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-22 Last comment 2026-10-26 ⏱ No recent activity [RFC PATCH 0/1] mm/swap: keep reclaim batches contiguous on swap
Show 1 individual patches
  • [1/1] mm/swap: allocate contiguous swap slots for a reclaim batch Neutral Submitted 2026-10-22Last comment 2026-10-23 Nhat Pham
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch aims to reduce the number of I/O requests on a swap device by keeping reclaim batches contiguous. The current implementation of swap allocation and deallocation can lead to scattered writes, resulting in increased I/O requests. The patch modifies the allocator to allocate contiguous free slots for a reclaim batch, reducing the number of small requests and improving throughput. This is achieved by informing the allocator about the size of the batch and allowing it to allocate contiguous slots accordingly. The goal is to reduce the overhead of swap I/O operations and improve system performance.

5 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kairui Song ↳ replying to Alexandre Ghiti Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Kairui Song suggested reverting commit 9a42aed48421, which prefers nonfull over free clusters for small order allocations, and instead always scanning full clusters to prioritize contiguity on large physical swap devices.
Show original comment 
Hi Alexandre,

That's a interesting problem. I think the real problem is with commit
9a42aed48421 ("mm, swap: prefer nonfull over free clusters"), right?

It's more like a balance between fragmentation and contiguity of small
order allocation issue.

Perhaps we should instead always scan the full clusters and revert
that one commit? That commit was only following the idea of keeping
the allocation more compacted for that series, it looked good at that
time. Since fragmentation issues can be solved in other ways,
contiguity seems to matter more for large physical swap devices.
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Alexandre Ghiti ↳ replying to Kairui Song Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that the patch does not handle the case where a reclaim batch spans multiple non-full clusters, which can lead to scattered writes and readahead requests. Reviewer Alexandre Ghiti expressed interest in the proposed solution and asked for more information about alternative fragmentation solutions.
Show original comment 
Hi Kairui,

On Thu, Oct 22, 2026 at 5:27 PM Kairui Song <ryncsn@gmail.com> wrote:

---

Oh, okay. I thought reverting it wasn't an option given the good
numbers the patch provides.

What are the other fragmentation solutions you are referring to?

Thanks,

Alex
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes, inquiry, request_for_info
Nhat Pham ↳ replying to Kairui Song Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Nhat Pham expressed concerns that the proposed solution of allocating contiguous swap slots may not address the underlying issue and could lead to increased memory overhead, scanning overhead, etc., suggesting that physical swap space compaction is still a problem and virtual swap space would just shift the issue.
Show original comment 
Hmm what other way are you thinking of? Physical swap space compaction
requires vswap, which has not landed. And we'd just be kicking the
problem to virtual swap space. Sure, we won't be failing allocation,
but that would still increase the memory overhead, scanning overhead,
etc.

I encountered this myself. Remember when I wrote my own swap allocator
for vswap? In one of the version, I went for free clusters first
instead of partial clusters. It fragmented the swap space, so I had to
copy your homework and went for partially used clusters first...

Anyway, maybe I'm wrong - some numbers would be nice here.
View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes, concerns
Nhat Pham Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Nhat Pham expressed support for the patch, noting that there are still workloads that benefit more from disk swapping than compression swapping, and requested that the swap subsystem be made to work for them. The reviewer agrees with the patch's approach of allocating contiguous swap slots, noting that it makes sense to not apply the heuristic for large folios. Reviewer Nhat Pham noted that the patch's approach of preferring non-full clusters over free clusters may lead to increased lock contention and fragmentation in swap files, but believes it will be fine as long as contiguity is found for the first time in the batch. Reviewer Nhat Pham questioned the effectiveness of the patch's approach, noting that it may constantly overshoot the estimate of pages to be reclaimed due to rotating/promoting references, which could create problems down the line. He suggested collecting a batch of pages after unmapping and passing them to the phys swap allocator as an alternative solution.
Show original comment 
Thanks for looking into this! There are still workloads that benefit
more from disk swapping than compression swapping... We should make
the swap subsystem work for them.

I haven't read the code too closely, as it's an RFC meant to show the
general idea. So I'll just make some comments and ask some questions
about the idea:

---

Ah so we don't invoke this heuristics for folio larger than certain
orders? Makes sense.

---

So we prefer non-full clusters with contiguous slots, over free
clusters, over other nonfull clusters.

I think this part makes sense to me FWIW. We do skip fast path and go
for slow paths more often, so do make sure we're not paying with too
much lock contention, especially as the swapfile fils or get more
fragmented (which this patch series does nudge us towards). But FWIW,
we pretty much go back to the old behavior the moment we fail to find
contiguity for the first time in the batch, so I think we'll be
fine...?

---

Some general questions:

1. How many pages in folio_list typically do get reclaimed? There can
be quite a few with references, which we rotate/promote, when we scan,
right? Would constantly overshooting the estimate create problem down
the line?

(This is one of the few places where indirection (vswap) would have
helped - we can collect a batch of pages after unmapping, then pass
them to the phys swap allocator (after checking zswappability, etc.).
Maybe we can solve it better after it's landed, and accept an
imperfect solution for now?)

2. Should we skip this heuristics for cgroups that enables zswap (and
maybe zram)? Contiguity matters less there... unless it help at
writeback time? Hard to say without experimentation - maybe you can do
the same check you did for your zswap writeback batching patch series?
View on lore ↗
Signals: support, request, concern about lock contention
Kairui Song ↳ replying to Alexandre Ghiti Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer suggested limiting the patch's scope to SYNC devices or exploring simpler solutions to address the problem, expressing skepticism about the significance of a 4% reduction in mTHP failures. Reviewer Kairui Song suggested addressing swap space fragmentation by sorting clusters by slot size before moving them back to the non-full list, which would mitigate the issue until a more comprehensive solution is implemented.
Show original comment 
That patch is 1/3 of a series, that series yields really nice
results, but that one patch plays only a small part in them. Or
perhaps we can limit that to SYNC devices? I hope there are simpler
ways to avoid the problem you encountered. I think the ~4% reduction
of 64k mTHP failure is kind of trivial compared to the problem you
encountered, and 2M swapout is still not easily doable, as the
main blocker is from the page allocator.

---

Right, nothing has landed in upstream for that yet, but with larger
swap space the fragmentation issue will be largely solved. And before
that we might be able to sort the clusters by the continues size of
slots on it instead just blindly move then back to nonfull on any
freeing.

Dmitry Ilvokhin

13 items
Patches Submitted (11)
Last comment 2026-10-28 [PATCH 00/10] treewide: switch to __nonnull_args() macro Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series replaces open-coded uses of the __attribute__((nonnull)) macro with the __nonnull_args() macro, which is a wrapper that keeps the quirks of the attribute in one place. The goal is to standardize nonnull attribute usage across the Linux kernel tree. The patches can be applied separately and are build-tested on various configurations. Some instances of __attribute__((nonnull)) remain in the tools subdirectory and should be updated separately after this patchset lands.

18 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Dmitry Ilvokhin (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about kernel-doc warnings by adding support for __nonnull_args() as a function transform, which will be used in the treewide migration from __attribute__((nonnull)) to __nonnull_args(). This change is preparatory and does not yet address the main patchset.
Show original comment 
Avoid kernel-doc warnings by supporting __nonnull_args()
as a function transform.

This is a preparatory change for the treewide migration from
__attribute__((nonnull)) to __nonnull_args() macro.

Signed-off-by: Dmitry Ilvokhin <d@ilvokhin.com>

Dmitry Ilvokhin (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about the tools subdirectory not having the __nonnull_args() macro defined, which was causing test builds to break. They added a definition for the macro in tools/include/linux/compiler.h.
Show original comment 
Commit 22302af28d3f ("cleanup: Annotate guard constructors with
nonnull") added the macro __nonnull_args() to wrap raw compiler
attribute.

Replace __attribute__((nonnull)) with __nonnull_args() to standardize
nonnull attribute usage across the tree and keep the attribute's quirks
in one place rather than open-coded at every call site.

tools/testing/shared/linux/xarray.h directly includes the kernel's
include/linux/xarray.h, which now uses __nonnull_args(). The macro is
not defined under tools/, breaking test builds.

[PATCH 04/10] xarray: switch to __nonnull_args() macro Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch standardizes the use of non-null attribute across the Linux kernel by replacing raw compiler attributes with a macro called __nonnull_args(). The macro is defined in tools/include/linux/compiler.h and used to annotate functions that require non-null arguments. This change ensures consistency in attribute usage and keeps quirks in one place, making it easier to maintain and update the codebase.

Dmitry Ilvokhin Neutral LLM
Provided additional context on the purpose of include/linux/compiler_attributes.h, explaining its role in making attribute wrappers simple and transparent to use.
Signals: NEUTRAL
Andy Shevchenko ↳ replying to Dmitry Ilvokhin Acked-by Positive LLM
Acknowledged Dmitry's additional context, suggesting that all headers pulling compiler_types/compile_attributes directly should be cleaned up.
Show original comment 
On Thu, Oct 29, 2026 at 01:12:06PM +0000, Dmitry Ilvokhin wrote:
> On Thu, Oct 29, 2026 at 02:00:28PM +0100, Miguel Ojeda wrote:
> > On Thu, Oct 29, 2026 at 8:32\u202fAM Thomas Zimmermann <tzimmermann@suse.de> wrote:
> > >
> > > Should gem.h now include <linux/compiler_attributes.h> ?
> > 
> > No, it should be included for you (unless you are doing something
> > custom with the compiler flags etc.).
> 
> Just to add a bit more colour to Miguel's reply.
> 
> I believe idea behind include/linux/compiler_attributes.h is to make
> attribute wrappers as simple and transparent to use as native compiler
> attributes. In particular, this means they should be available in every
> kernel compilation unit without any additional effort.
> 
> For this reason, all the nessesary plumbing is handled at the build
> machinery level (see scripts/Makefile.lib:171), where '-include'
> compiler flag is used to pull in include/linux/compiler_types.h, which
> in turn pulls include/linux/compiler_attributes.h.

Ah, TIL! Please, discard my comment then. Perhaps we need to clean up all
headers that pull compiler_types/compile_attributes directly (the same way
we do for kconfig.h).

With Best Regards,
Andy Shevchenko

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-16 Last comment 2026-10-22 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH 0/6] daxctl: use dax kmem atomic whole-device hotplug 6 patches
Show 6 individual patches
  • [1/6] libdaxctl: add dax kmem atomic hotplug state accessors Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
  • [2/6] daxctl: online whole-device memory atomically when available Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-22 Alison Schofield Gregory (Meta) (author)
  • [3/6] daxctl: offline whole-device memory via atomic unplug when available Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-22 Alison Schofield Gregory (Meta) (author)
  • [4/6] daxctl: report whole-device hotplug state in 'daxctl list' Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
  • [5/6] Documentation: describe atomic whole-device hotplug in daxctl man pages Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-21 Alison Schofield
  • [6/6] test/daxctl-devices.sh: cycle offline/online to cover unplug and replug Neutral Submitted 2026-10-16Last comment 2026-10-29 Alison Schofield Gregory (Meta) (author)
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series introduces support for atomic whole-device hotplug in daxctl using the dax_kmem attribute. This allows for safe and concurrent online and offline operations on a device, preventing races between agents like udev and auto-online. The patch also updates the 'daxctl list' command to report the hotplug state of devices, which can be inconsistent if blocks are toggled individually. The changes maintain compatibility with older daxctl versions and handle cases where unplug or re-adding fails due to lack of memory hotremove support or region reservation issues.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Alison Schofield Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Reviewer Alison Schofield noted that the patch series is missing an ndctl label, specifically [ndctl PATCH v2 0/6] from the GitHub repository, and requested it be added to align with kernel functionality introduced in version 7.3.

On Fri, Oct 16, 2026 at 05:42:42PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> Kernels with the dax_kmem daxX.Y/state attribute [1] can unplug or
> plug-and-online a whole device atomically. Use it in daxctl
> online-memory and offline-memory so other agents (udev, auto-online)
> can't race daxctl block by block, notably the offline-then-unbind race
> on teardown.
> 
> Kernels without the attribute keep the per-block path.
> 
> Behavior change: offline-memory now always unplugs the device (state
> 'unplugged'), removing its memory blocks, even if they were all
> offline already. Previously that was a no-op.
> 
> 'daxctl list' (and 'ndctl/cxl list -X') gains 'hotplug_state',
> reporting daxX.Y/state. The kernel only updates that when written, so
> it goes stale if blocks are toggled individually.
> 
> If there is a mismatch between 'dax/state' and the blocks, then
> 'hotplug_state' reports 'inconsistent' (online state / zones disagree).
> That is intentional: it flags agents fighting over the device.
> It only appears when the attribute exists.
> 
> Compatibility notes:
> - Where the unplug fails (e.g. no memory hotremove support),
>   online-memory falls back to the per-block path and offline-memory
>   of an already offline device remains a no-op.
> - Older daxctl/ndctl/cxl binaries don't expect a device with no memory
>   blocks.  Basically multiple daxctl versions on the same box can cause
>   older versions to fail when in the unplugged state.  Not a huge issue.
> - If re-adding fails after an unplug (e.g. region can't be reserved)
>   the device is left unplugged.
> - For multi-range devices the kernel succeeds if any range is added.
>   daxctl can't tell that a range is missing.
> 
> [1] https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260712154505.3564379-1-gourry@gourry.net/

On Fri, Oct 16, 2026 at 05:42:43PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> From: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>
> 
> Recent kernels add /sys/bus/dax/devices/daxX.Y/state to dax_kmem
> devices for atomic whole-device hotplug. It reads 'unplugged',
> 'offline', 'online', 'online_kernel' or 'online_movable', and accepts
> all but 'offline'.
> 
> Add an enum and get/set accessors for it. The getter returns
> DAXCTL_KMEM_UNKNOWN when the attribute is absent, so callers can fall
> back to per-block operations.

On Fri, Oct 16, 2026 at 05:42:44PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> From: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>
> 
> When the kernel provides daxX.Y/state, online a fully offline device
> by writing the online policy to it rather than onlining each block.
> This avoids racing other agents (udev, auto-online) that online blocks
> as they appear. The kernel only onlines from 'unplugged', so a device
> in 'offline' is unplugged first; if that fails (e.g. no memory
> hotremove support), the device is unchanged and is onlined per-block.
> 
> Memory onlined by another agent is left alone: the 'offline' case
> re-checks that no block is online before unplugging, and losing a
> racing online falls back to the per-block path. A small window remains
> between that check and the unplug.
> 
> The per-block path now fails when the device has no memory blocks,
> rather than reporting zero sections already online.
> 
> Signed-off-by: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>

> +		target = DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE;
> +	else
> +		target = DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE_MOVABLE;

Hi Greg, 

A couple of things here...

The atomic --no-movable path uses DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE, leaving zone selection
to the kernel. With memory_hotplug.online_policy=auto-movable, IIUC this could
place memory in ZONE_MOVABLE despite --no-movable.

The per-block path checks the resulting zone and fails if it isn't ZONE_NORMAL,
but the atomic path skips that check.

Should this use DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE_KERNEL instead, or retain DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE
and add the same zone check?


> +	if (state == DAXCTL_KMEM_OFFLINE) {
> +		/*
> +		 * Onlining requires 'unplugged'. Don't unplug memory another
> +		 * agent onlined meanwhile (a small window remains).
> +		 */

With this, a "reconfigure-device -m system-ram" may add the memory twice.
First kmem adds it offline at probe and then daxctl unplugs it and adds it
back online. If that re-add fails, the device is left unplugged rather than
offline.

I get that falling back to per-block onlining would reopen the race this
series is addressing. Could the kernel interface support transitioning
directly from offline to an online state instead? It seems that would avoid
both the extra hotplug work and the left-unplugged failure case.

-- Alison

On Fri, Oct 16, 2026 at 05:42:45PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> From: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>
> 
> When the kernel provides daxX.Y/state, offline by writing 'unplugged'.
> The kernel offlines and removes the whole device atomically, so no
> agent can online blocks before a later unbind. On failure the device
> is left unchanged.
> 
> The memory blocks are removed rather than left offline, including
> when they are all offline already; 'daxctl online-memory' restores
> them. If they are all offline and the unplug fails (e.g. no memory
> hotremove support), this remains a no-op.
> 
> Unplugging a node's last memory removes its sysfs directory, so treat
> a missing node directory as zero blocks when the device is unplugged.
> 'daxctl list' likewise omits 'movable' for a device with no blocks.
>

Hi Greg,

I see you called this out in the cover letter, and that the kernel is
deliberately combining offlining and removing memory to avoid the race
before unbind.

But, this does change the behavior of the command "daxctl offline-memory".
Previously it left memory blocks present but offline and now it removes
them entirely.

Should offline-memory keep its existing behavior of leaving memory blocks
offline, and use atomic unplug only when reconfiguring the device?



>  	num_off = num_sections - num_on;
> -	if (num_off == num_sections) {
> +	if (num_off == num_sections && !unplug) {
>  		fprintf(stderr, "%s: all memory sections (%d) already offline\n",
>  			devname, num_off);
>  		return 1;
>  	}
> -	if (num_off)
> +	if (num_off && num_on)
>  		fprintf(stderr, "%s: %d memory section%s already offline\n",
>  			devname, num_off,
>  			num_off == 1 ? "" : "s");
>  
> +	if (unplug) {
> +		rc = daxctl_dev_set_kmem_state(dev, DAXCTL_KMEM_UNPLUGGED);
> +		if (rc < 0 && num_on == 0) {
> +			/* already offline, unplug is best effort */

When num_on == 0, another agent could online a block between the earlier
check and the unplug and that memory would then be removed.

I see this noted in the commit message. Is that worth documenting for
users?


-- Alison

On Fri, Oct 16, 2026 at 05:42:48PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> From: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>
> 
> Repeat the online/offline pair to cover onlining from 'unplugged', and
> check that offline-memory unplugs an already offline device. After each
> step, check block counts and, where present, daxX.Y/state and the
> 'hotplug_state' reported by 'daxctl list'.

Hi Greg,

Please change how the tests handle the absence of the new sysfs attribute.
If a test expects the new interface, a missing attribute should cause
the test to fail, with a clear error message. We have had problems in
the past with tests silently not executing letting defects go undetected.

Note that we don't require backward compatibility for the unit tests.
The utilities must continue working with older kernels, but tests for new
functionality should fail loudly when that functionality is missing.
(I'm aware that not all tests follow that guideline, and they need fixing.)

Please also leave the existing test cases as they were and run the new
state-dependent cases last.

A few stale state cases would exercise daxctl behavior that the kernel
selftest can't cover - 

  - Offline one memory block out-of-band, verify that daxctl list
    reports hotplug_state "inconsistent", then verify that
    offline-memory still leaves the device unplugged with its blocks
    removed.

  - Offline all blocks out-of-band, then verify that
    "reconfigure-device -m devdax" succeeds and the blocks are removed.

  - With all blocks offline but the device state still reporting
    online, verify that online-memory falls back to the per-block path
    and brings everything online.

For "blocks removed", please check that the device's memory blocks
are gone from /sys/devices/system/memory.  ie verify in sysfs directly
independent of the libdaxctl code being tested.

About multi-range devices-

I found that libdaxctl undercounts memory blocks on multi-range devices.
I'm posting a separate fix and test for that. Nothing in this series needs
to change. I'll cc you for review.

Our ndctl emulation can't exercise multi-range hotplug, but the kernel
selftest can run against a real multi-range device. Could you add coverage
there, including the partial-add case where the kernel reports success
even though not all ranges were added?

-- Alison

On Thu, Oct 22, 2026 at 12:46:40PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> On 2026-10-21 19:20 -0700, Alison Schofield wrote:
> > On Fri, Oct 16, 2026 at 05:42:45PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> > > +	/*
> > > +	 * Unplug atomically where supported, even if already offline, so
> > > +	 * no agent can online blocks before a later unbind.
> > > +	 */
> > > +	state = daxctl_dev_get_kmem_state(dev);
> > > +	unplug = state != DAXCTL_KMEM_UNKNOWN && state != DAXCTL_KMEM_UNPLUGGED;
> > > +
> > 
> > Hi Greg,
> > 
> > I see you called this out in the cover letter, and that the kernel is
> > deliberately combining offlining and removing memory to avoid the race
> > before unbind.
> > 
> > But, this does change the behavior of the command "daxctl offline-memory".
> > Previously it left memory blocks present but offline and now it removes
> > them entirely.
> > 
> > Should offline-memory keep its existing behavior of leaving memory blocks
> > offline, and use atomic unplug only when reconfiguring the device?
> >
> 
> The kernel explicitly does not allow `echo offline > dax/state` because
> this just creates the race we're trying to resolve.
> 
> I suppose my pushback is: why does it matter that daxctl offline-memory
> changes in this way? If daxctl supports the atomic interface, it all just
> works. It's a bit more work (additional hot-unplug) but if the memory is
> already offline that's not that expensive.
> 
> The only scenario I could come up with is having two versions of daxctl
> fight with each other (old - no awareness of dax/state, new - uses
> dax/state) but is this a real concern?

My concern isn't about 2 version of daxctl fighting. It's about
preserving the documented behavior for "daxctl offline-memory". And just
generally, if there is no need to change it, rather not change it.

>From the daxctl offline-memory man page:
If all blocks were already offline, print a message and return success
without actually doing anything.

It seems the benefit of atomic unplug is w "daxctl reconfigure-device"
path where memory needs to be removed before unbinding dax_kmem. Could
we limit atomic unplug to that path?

To be clear, I don't know if that (limiting) is doable and I also don't
know if any current user would care either way. I'm just defending the
documentated behavior (FWIW).

-- Alison

Author acknowledged a concern about 'daxctl offline-memory' changing its behavior to remove memory blocks entirely when using atomic unplug, explained that this is intentional and aligns with the kernel's policy of not allowing concurrent block/state changes. Author pushed back on the idea that this change matters, suggesting it's a minor issue and that if daxctl supports the atomic interface, everything works as expected.

> Hi Greg, 
> 
> A couple of things here...
> 
> The atomic --no-movable path uses DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE, leaving zone selection
> to the kernel. With memory_hotplug.online_policy=auto-movable, IIUC this could
> place memory in ZONE_MOVABLE despite --no-movable.
> 
> The per-block path checks the resulting zone and fails if it isn't ZONE_NORMAL,
> but the atomic path skips that check.
> 
> Should this use DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE_KERNEL instead, or retain DAXCTL_KMEM_ONLINE
> and add the same zone check?

I think you are right, let me go test.

> 
> > +	if (state == DAXCTL_KMEM_OFFLINE) {
> > +		/*
> > +		 * Onlining requires 'unplugged'. Don't unplug memory another
> > +		 * agent onlined meanwhile (a small window remains).
> > +		 */
> 
> With this, a "reconfigure-device -m system-ram" may add the memory twice.
> First kmem adds it offline at probe and then daxctl unplugs it and adds it
> back online. If that re-add fails, the device is left unplugged rather than
> offline.
>

This was the intended behavior because otherwise a concurrent
block/state change would end up making the blocks unrecoverable.

> I get that falling back to per-block onlining would reopen the race this
> series is addressing. Could the kernel interface support transitioning
> directly from offline to an online state instead? It seems that would avoid
> both the extra hotplug work and the left-unplugged failure case.
> 

This is an unfortunate wart for backward compatibility sake.  It would
have been preferable to create the dax device without auto-hotplugging
the kmem region (hotplug but offline), but that would break userspace.

I can take another look whether i can get offline->online to work
internally, but this may still require the hotplug work you're concerned
about.

On 2026-10-22 15:00 -0700, Alison Schofield wrote:
> On Thu, Oct 22, 2026 at 12:46:40PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> > On 2026-10-21 19:20 -0700, Alison Schofield wrote:
> > > On Fri, Oct 16, 2026 at 05:42:45PM -0400, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> > The only scenario I could come up with is having two versions of daxctl
> > fight with each other (old - no awareness of dax/state, new - uses
> > dax/state) but is this a real concern?
> 
> My concern isn't about 2 version of daxctl fighting. It's about
> preserving the documented behavior for "daxctl offline-memory". And just
> generally, if there is no need to change it, rather not change it.
> 
> From the daxctl offline-memory man page:
> If all blocks were already offline, print a message and return success
> without actually doing anything.
> 
> It seems the benefit of atomic unplug is w "daxctl reconfigure-device"
> path where memory needs to be removed before unbinding dax_kmem. Could
> we limit atomic unplug to that path?
> 
> To be clear, I don't know if that (limiting) is doable and I also don't
> know if any current user would care either way. I'm just defending the
> documentated behavior (FWIW).
> 

hm, maybe instead:

daxctl offline-memory      - stays exactly as-is
daxctl unplug-memory       - does the unplug
daxctl reconfigure-device  - converts to unplug-memory under the hood

userland that is suffering from these races can update their scripts
from offline-memory to unplug-memory, but if they're getting it from
reconfigure-device, they get it for free.

Unfortunately this is the painful result of `offline` having been the
default hotplug state when dax/kmem gets probed, instead of `unplugged`.
So we're a bit stuck with the `offline -> unplugged -> online` pattern.

The intent here was to try to get drivers creating dax/kmem to actively
set a default and effectively deprecate the global hotplug auto-online
setting (because a single auto-online setting for all devices does not
make much sense).

I intended to follow up the series with a CXL build parameter that lets
a system owner set the default, but omit "offline" and instead make them
choose between unplugged and some form of online.

I think that resolves the daxctl problem entirely more or less?

-- 
~Gregory

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-24 Last comment 2026-10-26 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] mm/mempolicy: recompute automatic interleave weights on memory hotplug Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch updates the automatic interleave weights in Linux's memory policy when a node gains or loses memory due to hotplug events. The current implementation only recalculates the weights when a new bandwidth is reported, which can lead to incorrect weights being used for some nodes. This patch introduces a new function `wi_state_refresh` that recomputes the weights from the recorded bandwidth whenever a node gains its first memory or loses its last. This ensures that the weighted interleave algorithm always uses accurate and up-to-date weights, leading to improved performance in certain scenarios.

Last comment 2026-10-30 [PATCH v2] mm/mempolicy: recompute automatic interleave weights on memory hotplug Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a performance regression caused by outdated automatic interleave weights in the Linux kernel's memory policy module (mm/mempolicy). When nodes gain or lose memory, their weights are not recalculated from their reported bandwidths, leading to an uneven distribution of data and potential overload. The patch introduces a new function, wi_state_refresh(), which recomputes the weights based on the recorded bandwidth whenever a node gains or loses its first/last memory. If allocating new weights fails, it warns once and continues with the next recompute starting from the full bandwidth table.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

+static int wi_state_node_notifier(struct notifier_block *nb,
+				  unsigned long action, void *data)
+{
+	struct node_notify *nn = data;
+	int err;
+

+	    action != NODE_REMOVED_LAST_MEMORY)
+		return NOTIFY_OK;
+
+	/* The hotplug is done, failing the notification would not undo it */

+		pr_warn_once("failed to recompute weighted interleave weights for node%d: %d\n",
+			     nn->nid, err);
+	return NOTIFY_OK;

Last comment 2026-10-30 [PATCH] mm/mempolicy: use one node set for interleave weights Neutral LLM

This patch addresses a divide error on x86 and panic when memory is being online, caused by torn reads on the nodemask N_MEMORY. It records the nodes visited while summing bandwidth and uses that same set for scaling and reducing weights, ensuring consistent state even if N_MEMORY changes.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Joshua Hahn Reviewed-by Positive LLM
Approved the fix and added a Reviewed-by tag, considering it makes sense to use a consistent node set for interleave weights.
Show original comment 
On Fri, Oct 30, 2026 at 12:12 PM Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net> wrote:
>
> From: Andrew Morton <akpm@linux-foundation.org>
>
> From: Andrew Morton <akpm@linux-foundation.org>
>
> Switching weighted interleave back to automatic mode while memory is
> being onlined can cause a kernel divide error on x86, and a panic if
> panic_on_oops is set.
>
> reduce_interleave_weights() scans N_MEMORY three times, but hotplug
> can cause N_MEMORY to change at runtime - and torn reads on that
> nodemask can cause a total sum of zero bandwidth.  A node that
> was hot-unplugged and then hotplugged back can then cause this
> loop to generate a div/0.
>
> Record the nodes visited while summing their bandwidth, then use
> that same set when scaling and reducing the weights.  This gives
> us a consistent state even if N_MEMORY changes.

Makes a lot of sense to me. Thanks for the fix!
Reviewed-by: Joshua Hahn <joshua.hahnjy@gmail.com>

> Fixes: e341f9c3c841 ("mm/mempolicy: Weighted Interleave Auto-tuning")
> Cc: <stable@vger.kernel.org>
> Signed-off-by: Andrew Morton <akpm@linux-foundation.org>
> Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
Signals: LGTM
Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-19 Last comment 2026-10-26 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH] mm/vmscan: reclaim every zone in per-node proactive reclaim Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the per-node proactive reclaim function to scan every zone, not just ZONE_NORMAL, in order to reclaim movable memory as well. This addresses a problem where nodes with only movable memory were not being reclaimed properly. The approach taken is to change the `reclaim_idx` field of the `scan_control` structure to always set it to the maximum number of zones, allowing all zones to be scanned.

[PATCH v4 0/8] mm: fix allocator races with memory hotplug 8 patches

  • [4/8] mm/mmzone: read each zoneref slot once when filtering by nodemask
  • [5/8] mm/page_alloc: retry allocations that raced with a zonelist rebuild
  • [6/8] mm/hugetlb: retry the pool walk when the zonelist is rebuilt
  • [7/8] mm/page_alloc: retry alloc_contig_pages() when the zonelist is rebuilt
  • [8/8] mm/mempolicy: read the zoneref slot once in mempolicy_slab_node()
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses eight issues related to memory hotplug and allocator races in the Linux kernel. The problems include crashes, failures, or incorrect zone or node selection due to concurrent access and rebuilds of zonelists during memory hotplug operations. The patches fix these issues by addressing specific walkers that cause the problems, including allocation routines and memory policy functions. The fixes are designed to be stable and have been tested on a QEMU guest with three NUMA nodes and a 512M pc-dimm left offline at boot. The testing shows significant improvements in performance, with reduced instruction counts and page faults per second.

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses memory hotplug-related races and issues in the Linux kernel's memory allocator. The patches fix problems such as zone list rebuilds, page set freeing, and allocation failures that can occur when memory is added or removed from a system while allocations are happening. The approach taken involves fixing specific walkers and functions to ensure correct behavior during these operations, including removing a helper function that invites the same bug. The patches were tested on a QEMU guest with three NUMA nodes and showed improved results compared to an unmodified kernel.

Gregory (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing a concern about the retry mechanism in __alloc_pages_slowpath() not working correctly when can_retry_reserves is already set to false after a restart. The author agrees that this is an issue and proposes resetting can_retry_reserves on restart, ensuring that the retry runs at most once per change.
Show original comment 
Commit 2c4c3e29897d ("mm/page_alloc: simplify __alloc_pages_slowpath()
flow") retries an allocation once with reserve_flags when the caller
becomes eligible for reserves, guarded by can_retry_reserves.

A restart after a cpuset update or a zonelist rebuild retries with
can_retry_reserves already set to false, so a caller that cannot
reclaim (GFP_NOWAIT | __GFP_MEMALLOC), fails without trying reserves.

Reset can_retry_reserves on restart. Only a cpuset or zonelist change
restarts the slowpath, so the retry still runs at most once per change.

With a kprobe helping to make the race deterministic, an allocation
can be made to return NULL. With this patch the allocation will succeed.

Gregory (Meta) (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about the reclaim_retry_zone tracepoint dereferencing a NULL zone pointer when memory hotplug rebuilds zonelists in place. They agreed to pass the zone pointer instead of the zoneref pointer, which fixes the issue.
Show original comment 
From: Josef Bacik <josef@toxicpanda.com>

should_reclaim_retry() walks a zonelist and passes the zoneref pointer
to the reclaim_retry_zone tracepoint, which loads the zone from the
entry again to find the node.

Memory hotplug rebuilds the zonelists in place while allocations are
under way. When a zone in front of the zoneref entry has become empty,
the entries move up, and this one can be the end marker by the time the
tracepoint looks at it. zonelist_node_idx() then dereferences a NULL
zone. Short of that, the event can name another zone than the one its
numbers belong to.

Pass the zone pointer instead of the zoneref pointer.

2.56.0

Signals: agreed, fixes
Gregory (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern that the zonelist_update_seq retry was limited to CONFIG_MEMORY_HOTREMOVE, but this led to spurious allocation failures for __GFP_THISNODE callers when onlining zones. The author agreed to gate the sequence checks on CONFIG_MEMORY_HOTPLUG instead.
Show original comment 
The zonelist_update_seq retry was limited to CONFIG_MEMORY_HOTREMOVE,
presumably because only hot-remove takes zones out of a zonelist.

However, build_zonerefs_node() rewrites the zonelists in place, so
onlining the first block of a zone shifts the entries that follow it
in every zonelist containing that zone.  This can cause a spurious
allocation failure for a __GFP_THISNODE caller.

For a node with only ZONE_NORMAL, onlining its first ZONE_MOVABLE block
rewrites the node's ZONELIST_NOFALLBACK list as:

  [NORMAL, NULL] -> [MOVABLE, NULL] -> [MOVABLE, NORMAL, NULL]

A concurrent GFP_KERNEL | __GFP_THISNODE allocation can observe the
intermediate list, skip ZONE_MOVABLE, and find no eligible zone. With
CONFIG_MEMORY_HOTREMOVE=n, check_retry_zonelist() returns 0 and the
allocation fails even though the node has free ZONE_NORMAL memory.

Gate the sequence checks on CONFIG_MEMORY_HOTPLUG, which covers every
configuration that rebuilds zonelists after boot.

Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed
Gregory (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing a concern about the swapoff path dropping the per-vswap spinlock before calling try_to_unmap(). They acknowledged that this is necessary to prevent corruption of per-cpu memory, and explained that the pagesets are only freed because setup_zone_pageset() would otherwise leak them. The author plans to keep the pagesets instead and let setup_zone_pageset() use them again when the zone gets memory.
Show original comment 
From: Josef Bacik <josef@toxicpanda.com>

When the last memory of a zone goes offline, zone_pcp_reset() frees
zone->per_cpu_pageset and zone->per_cpu_zonestats. Nothing waits for those
who found the zone populated and still use the two pointers. They take
neither the memory hotplug lock nor any other lock which offline_pages()
holds, and some of them can sleep:

  CPU0 vmstat_update()                CPU1 offline_pages()
  refresh_cpu_vm_stats()
    for_each_populated_zone(zone)
      pcp = zone->per_cpu_pageset
      cond_resched()
                                      adjust_present_page_count()
                                      zone_pcp_reset(zone)
                                        free_percpu(zone->per_cpu_pageset)
      decay_pcp_high(zone, pcp)
      __this_cpu_write(pcp->expire, 0)

The same goes for drain_all_pages(NULL), which takes pcp->lock, for
zone_pcp_update(), show_free_areas() and the readers and writers of the
zonestats. If the per-cpu memory has got a new owner in the meantime, it
is corrupted.

The pagesets are only freed because setup_zone_pageset() would otherwise
leak them when the zone gets memory again. Keep them instead and let
setup_zone_pageset() and zone_pcp_init() use them again, like it is done
with pgdat->per_cpu_nodestats. Their lists are empty, and their limits
have been calculated for a zone without memory by the
init_per_zone_wmark_min() of offline_pages(), which is what
setup_zone_pageset() would come up with as well.

Signals: acknowledged a fix is needed, explained reasoning
Gregory (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about the compiler reordering loads of a zoneref slot, which could lead to using an incorrect zone or NULL pointer. They proposed using READ_ONCE() to ensure a single load and prevent this issue.
Show original comment 
Memory hotplug rebuilds zonelists in place while allocations walk them,
so a zoneref slot can change between two loads of it.

zonelist_zone() loads the slot with a plain access.  The compiler may
load it again later instead of keeping the zone it already read, so a
caller can check one zone and then use another, or the NULL terminator.

Use READ_ONCE().  Callers that take a zone from a slot and keep using
it, such as get_page_from_freelist() with ac->preferred_zoneref, need
that single load.

Fixes: 11cd8638c37f ("mm, page_alloc: remove stop_machine from build_all_zonelists")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

+	/* Memory hotplug rewrites zonelists in place. */
+	return READ_ONCE(zoneref->zone);
 }

Signals: acknowledges fix is needed
Gregory (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about the __next_zones_zonelist() function reading a slot twice, which can lead to a NULL pointer dereference when offlining a zone's last block. The author explained that current GCC and clang merge the two reads, but nothing requires it, and provided an example where holding a nodemask allocation between the reads causes an oops. They agreed to restructure the code in v2 to read the slot only once.
Show original comment 
__next_zones_zonelist() tests a slot's zone for NULL, then reads the
slot again to look up the zone's node. Offlining a zone's last block
rebuilds the zonelists in place, so the second read can return the
terminator:

  CPU 0                             CPU 1
  __next_zones_zonelist(nodemask)
    zonelist_zone(slot 1) = NORMAL
                                    offline_pages()
                                      build_all_zonelists()
                                        [MOVABLE, NORMAL, NULL]
                                        -> [NORMAL, NULL]
    zref_in_nodemask(slot 1)
      zone_to_nid(NULL)

Current GCC and clang merge the two reads, but nothing requires it.
With mm/mmzone.o built with -fno-inline -fno-ipa-sra, holding a
nodemask allocation between the reads while the node's last movable
block goes offline oopses every time:

  BUG: kernel NULL pointer dereference, address: 0000000000000060
  RIP: 0010:zone_to_nid+0x0/0x10
  Call Trace:
   zref_in_nodemask+0x9/0x20
   __next_zones_zonelist+0x23/0x70
   prepare_alloc_pages.constprop.0+0x16c/0x190
   __alloc_frozen_pages_noprof+0xaf/0x330

Read the slot once and test that zone. A NULL zone still ends the walk.

2.56.0
View on lore ↗
Signals: acknowledged fix needed, agreed to restructure
Gregory (Meta) (author) Needs Work LLM
The author addressed a concern about the allocator loading an ineligible zone during memory hotplug, explained that this can happen when a rebuild occurs during an allocation walk and proposed adding checks to free pages if the zonelist has changed since the last check. The author agreed to add these checks in future revisions.
Show original comment 
Onlining the first block of a zone rebuilds every zonelist in place,
shifting the slots after the new zone.  The iterator approves a slot,
then the allocator loads the zone from it, so a GFP_KERNEL request can
land in ZONE_MOVABLE and a nodemask request on a node outside its mask:

  CPU 0                             CPU 1
  prepare_alloc_pages()
    first_zones_zonelist(nodemask {1})
      slot 0 (NORMAL1) approved
                                    online_pages()
                                      build_all_zonelists()
                                        [NORMAL1, NORMAL2, ...]
                                        -> [MOVABLE1, NORMAL1, ...]
  get_page_from_freelist()
    zone = zonelist_zone(slot 0)    /* MOVABLE1 */
    allocates from MOVABLE1

zonelist_update_seq detects the rebuild, but only the slowpath's failure
exits check it.

Check it whenever an allocation succeeds too, and if it changed, free
the page and retry, as the memcg charge failure path does.  Eligible
pages are dropped as well: zonelists only change when memory comes or
goes, and a retry is simpler than rechecking the page.

Freeing the page does not undo a block the allocation claimed.  A
GFP_KERNEL request that lands in a fresh ZONE_MOVABLE converts a free
block to MIGRATE_UNMOVABLE, which stays until the zone's movable free
list runs out and a movable allocation takes it back.

The fast and bulk paths read the seqlock's raw seqcount without waiting:
an ALLOC_NOLOCK caller can be an NMI that interrupted the writer, and on
PREEMPT_RT the seqlock accessor takes the spinlock.  A sample taken
mid-write costs a retry, or NULL under ALLOC_NOLOCK.
check_retry_zonelist() returns 0 when compiled out, so a backport
without the hot-add change stays harmless.

Fixes: 11cd8638c37f ("mm, page_alloc: remove stop_machine from build_all_zonelists")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org

Reviewed-by: Vlastimil Babka (SUSE) <vbabka@kernel.org>

Signals: acknowledges fix needed
Gregory (Meta) (author) Positive LLM
The author addressed a concern about dequeue_hugetlb_folio_nodemask() taking a pool page from the wrong node by introducing a seqlock to protect zonelist updates and checking for retry before using each zone, agreeing that this change fixes the issue.
Show original comment 
Onlining the first block of a zone rebuilds every zonelist in place,
shifting the slots after the new zone.  dequeue_hugetlb_folio_nodemask()
approves a slot by its node, then loads the zone from it, so a hugetlb
fault bound to one node can take a pool page from another:

  CPU 0                             CPU 1
  dequeue_hugetlb_folio_nodemask(nodemask {2})
    slot 3 (NORMAL2) approved
                                    online_pages()
                                      build_all_zonelists()
                                        [.., NORMAL1, NORMAL2]
                                        -> [.., MOVABLE1, NORMAL1, ...]
    zone = zonelist_zone(slot 3)    /* NORMAL1 */
    dequeue_hugetlb_folio_node_exact(h, 1)

Check zonelist_update_seq before using each zone, and on the way out
with nothing found, and restart the walk if it changed.  Checking before
the dequeue leaves nothing to put back.  Reset the last-tried node on
restart, so it is not skipped.

With kprobes holding the walk across the online, a fault bound to node 2
took a node 1 page in 3 of 3 runs, and a node 2 page in 3 of 3 with this
change.

Fixes: 11cd8638c37f ("mm, page_alloc: remove stop_machine from build_all_zonelists")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>

Signals: acknowledged fix needed, agreed with approach
Gregory (Meta) (author) Neutral LLM
The author addressed a concern about concurrent zonelist rebuilds causing NULL or replaced preferred zone slots in get_page_from_freelist() and alloc_pages_bulk_noprof(). They explained that reading the slot once keeps a usable zone, as zones are never freed. The changes read the preferred zone only once and handle stale or NULL slots by failing the same way as walking off the end.
Show original comment 
get_page_from_freelist() and alloc_pages_bulk_noprof() reread
ac->preferred_zoneref->zone at every use. Offlining a zone's last block
rebuilds the zonelists in place, and the shrunk list's NULL terminator
can land on the preferred slot mid-walk:

  CPU 0                             CPU 1
  get_page_from_freelist(GFP_KERNEL | __GFP_THISNODE)
    zone = NORMAL (slot 1)
                                    offline_pages()
                                      build_all_zonelists()
                                        [MOVABLE, NORMAL, NULL]
                                        -> [NORMAL, NULL]
    rmqueue(zonelist_zone(slot 1), zone)
      zone_statistics(NULL, zone)

  BUG: kernel NULL pointer dereference, address: 0000000000000060
  RIP: 0010:get_page_from_freelist+0x6a5/0x15f0
  Call Trace:
   __alloc_frozen_pages_noprof+0x125/0x330

Read the slot once. Zones are never freed, so a stale zone is safe, and
a NULL slot fails the same way as walking off the end.

Fixes: 11cd8638c37f ("mm, page_alloc: remove stop_machine from build_all_zonelists")
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
Reviewed-by: Vlastimil Babka (SUSE) <vbabka@kernel.org>

Last comment 2026-10-27 [PATCH 0/6] mm: fix pfn walker races with memory offline 6 patches
Show 6 individual patches
  • [1/6] mm/compaction: wait for pfn walkers before poisoning offlined memmap Neutral Last comment 2026-10-27 David (Arm)
  • [2/6] mm/page_owner: hold off memory offline while a page is read
  • [3/6] mm/memory-failure: keep the page online while handling it
  • [4/6] mm/page_alloc: don't undo another isolation in alloc_contig_range Neutral Last comment 2026-10-27 David (Arm) Gregory (Meta) (author) Zi Yan Gregory Price (author)
  • [5/6] mm/page_alloc: keep the range online while alloc_contig isolates it
  • [6/6] mm/hwpoison-inject: keep the page online while filtering it
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses six races in the Linux kernel's memory management code that can cause crashes or incorrect behavior when memory is offline. The patches introduce a new function, pfn_walk, which waits for pending page walkers before poisoning the memory map, and modify various functions to use this new function or hold get_online_mems() instead. This ensures that memory is not accessed after it has been marked as offline, preventing crashes and incorrect behavior. The changes are tested on a QEMU guest with three NUMA nodes and a pc-dimm left offline at boot, and the results show that the patches fix all six races without introducing any significant performance cost.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
David (Arm) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer David from Arm expressed concerns that the patch series is low priority and should be discussed further, citing a lack of real-world reports and suggesting that the issues are not as likely to happen as thought. He also suggested using an RCU for pfn_to_online_page() + zone->contig thingies instead of get_online_mems(), and proposed adding infrastructure in a separate patch rather than manging it into a patch that fixes a DEBUG_VM splat.

> I actually have a reason for these fixes other than "bot said bad".

So you found them on production kernels?

We did not have a single real report of that for probably a decade or
even longer in production.

Recently, we had the single first report about the compaction case from a
stress test (no production):

https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260903095543.3752338-1-yuan1.liu@intel.com/

"We are not sure whether this race is reachable under realistic
workloads or only under this synthetic stress test."

Where I motivated that we need something clean.

> 
> We leverage ballooning and hotplugging as VM memory overcommit tools
> and are finding that some of these race conditions crash them with
> large MTBF.

yeah, that makes sense.

> 
> While most of these are insanely narrow, they are not as unlikely to
> happen as you think. A vCPU can take a vmexit in these windows much
> more easily than a host can take an NMI or other interrupt.

Jup, that's what I would expect.

> 
> Thankfully none of these (or the other series) appear to be obvious
> security issues, but they are stability issues.

Right. And DEBUG_VM reports are irrelevant in production.

The thing is that the race between offlining and actual memory removal
is extremely long. Poisoning is just a mechanism to find these theoretical
issues that could cause issues faster.

And with all the other things going on, this has lower priority, and I
wanted to get back to the AI patches I have once the other thing is sorted.

> 
> I have a reproducer for every single one of these (and the other
> series) - but a handful are unreachable due to a 2-statement read
> being emitted as a single read by the compiler (which can be undone
> by a targeted compiler flag). Those were included for completeness.


In general, I think we want something that will protect all
pfn_to_online_page() + zone->contig thingies, not just selected ones.
We discussed in the past using some sort of rcu for that.

get_online_mems is usually the wrong approach.

The infrastructure for that should be added in a clean separate patch, not
mangled into a patch that fixes a DEBUG_VM splat.

This is nothing to rush in or to schlop around.

The AI patch for compaction I have here does something similar to your first
patch, with a bit less churn, and converts a handful more users.

E.g.,


+/*
+ * Nothing keeps a section online once pfn_to_online_page() has returned. A pfn
+ * walker that holds no reference to the page does the check and looks at the
+ * memmap under this guard: remove_pfn_range_from_zone() waits for it before the
+ * memmap is poisoned and can be freed. That wait cannot be interrupted, and
+ * whatever a walker sleeps on under the guard, memory offlining waits for as
+ * well: leave the guard before copying to user space. mem_hotplug_lock is held
+ * during the wait: never take it under the guard.
+ */
+extern struct srcu_struct memmap_srcu;
+#define memmap_walk_guard()    guard(srcu)(&memmap_srcu)


and then

+static u64 kpage_info(unsigned long pfn, enum kpage_operation op)
+{
+       struct page *page;
+
+       /* The page is not ours: keep its memmap until we are done with it. */
+       memmap_walk_guard();
+
+       /*
+        * TODO: ZONE_DEVICE support requires to identify
+        * memmaps that were actually initialized.
+        */
+       page = pfn_to_online_page(pfn);
+       if (!page)
+               return 0;
+
+       switch (op) {
+       case KPAGE_FLAGS:
+               return stable_page_flags(page);
+       case KPAGE_COUNT:
+               return get_kpage_count(page);
+       case KPAGE_CGROUP:
+               return page_cgroup_ino(page);
+       default:
+               return 0;
+       }
+}

On 10/27/26 05:22, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> An alloc_contig_range() that overlaps a block going offline can leave
> the offline spinning until it gets a signal, holding the device hotplug
> lock.  When isolation fails, alloc_contig_frozen_range() undoes it over
> the whole range, clearing the offline's MIGRATE_ISOLATE too:
> 
>   CPU 1 (offline_pages)               CPU 2 (alloc_contig_range)
>   start_isolate_page_range()
>     blocks are MIGRATE_ISOLATE
>                                       start_isolate_page_range()
>                                         -EBUSY on CPU 1's blocks
>                                       undo_isolate_page_range()
>                                         blocks are MIGRATE_MOVABLE
>   test_pages_isolated()
>     never succeeds again
> 
> start_isolate_page_range() fails only on a block someone else isolated,
> after undoing its own, so this undo only ever clears another caller's.
> Return directly, as before alloc_contig_range() went pageblock granular.
> 
> With offline_pages() parked after isolating a DIMM block and a 1G
> hugetlb allocation made over it, the offline on base still loops 30s
> later in 4 of 4 runs:
> 
>   offline_pages+0x5b2/0x1570
>   memory_subsys_offline+0x17e/0x470
>   device_offline+0x250/0x340
>   state_store+0x88/0x110
> 
> With this patch the allocation still fails, and the offline completes in
> 3 of 3 runs.
> 
> Fixes: b2c9e2fbba32 ("mm: make alloc_contig_range work at pageblock granularity")
> Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
> Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
> ---

We have an existing AI fix for that as well already handled by Vlasta.

I think we'll have to establish some model of "ask David first before posting"
because otherwise we just create duplicate work for everyone.

-- 
Cheers,

David

On 10/27/26 05:21, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> Compaction checks that a pageblock is online, then uses its pages across
> lock drops and sleeps.  If offline poisons the memmap meanwhile, a
> DEBUG_VM kernel faults:
> 
>   CPU 1 (compaction)                  CPU 2 (offline_pages)
>   pageblock_pfn_to_page()
>     block is online
>   isolate_migratepages_block()
>     cond_resched()
>                                       offline_mem_sections()
>                                       remove_pfn_range_from_zone()
>                                         page_init_poison()
>     set_pageblock_skip(valid_page)
>       reads the zone of a poisoned page
> 
>   Oops: general protection fault, probably for non-canonical address 0xdffffc00000006a2
>   RIP: 0010:set_pfnblock_bit+0xa6/0x2b0
>    isolate_migratepages_block+0x2456/0x3c80
>    compact_zone+0x1638/0x3900
>    compact_node+0x12a/0x1f0
>    sysctl_compaction_handler+0xec/0x160
> 
> The free scanner faults the same way.  A contiguous zone skips the
> online check, since offline clears zone->contiguous only after
> poisoning.
> 
> Hold an SRCU-fast read section (pfn_walk) from the block check to the
> last use of its pages, and make remove_pfn_range_from_zone() clear
> zone->contiguous and wait for it before poisoning.  Offline pays one
> grace period per range.
> 
> Scanner positions outlive the section, and offline can shrink the zone
> meanwhile, so take the contiguous fast path only inside the current
> span, publish zone->contiguous with release, and keep the migrate
> scanner inside the zone.
> 
> With a kprobe parking the migrate scanner across the offline, base
> faults in 3 of 3 runs and this patch in none.  Unforced offline/online
> cycling under compaction faults in 8 of 24 runs on base and 0 of 12 with
> this patch.
> 
> Fixes: d33695b16a9f ("mm/memory_hotplug: poison memmap in remove_pfn_range_from_zone()")
> Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
> Signed-off-by: Gregory Price (Meta) <gourry@gourry.net>
> ---

BTW, the order of these patches is all wrong.

There are much more important fixes hiding in this series. And this change here
would certainly not be the first thing we'd backport.

Please create a proper series that only addresses the pfn_to_online_page()
situation, without different, more urgent fixes.

Because there is no way we will fast track anything like this here into stable
kernels.

-- 
Cheers,

David

View on lore ↗
Signals: low priority, suggested alternative approach
Gregory (Meta) (author) Positive LLM
The author is addressing David Hildenbrand's (Arm) feedback that the patch series appears low-priority and not based on real-world issues, as well as his suggestion to discuss this further outside of the merge window. The author explains that they have a reason for these fixes beyond 'bot said bad', citing production kernel crashes and hangs caused by the race conditions in ballooning and hotplugging VM memory overcommit tools. They also mention that they have reproducers for every single one of these issues, but some are unreachable due to compiler optimizations.
Show original comment 
On 2026-10-27 09:57 +0100, David Hildenbrand (Arm) wrote:
> On 10/27/26 05:21, Gregory Price (Meta) wrote:
> > Several pfn walkers check that a struct page is online, then use it
> > after memory offline has poisoned it (CONFIG_DEBUG_VM) or removal has
> > freed the vmemmap.  Five of the races crash the kernel; the sixth leaves
> > an offline spinning with the device hotplug lock held.
> 
> I have roughly the exact same list of AI garbage fixes here from a certain company.
> 
> And I consider this all extremely low priority.
> 
> So this is something to discuss throughout the next months and do it properly.
> 

I actually have a reason for these fixes other than "bot said bad".

We leverage ballooning and hotplugging as VM memory overcommit tools
and are finding that some of these race conditions crash them with
large MTBF.

While most of these are insanely narrow, they are not as unlikely to
happen as you think. A vCPU can take a vmexit in these windows much
more easily than a host can take an NMI or other interrupt.

Thankfully none of these (or the other series) appear to be obvious
security issues, but they are stability issues.

I have a reproducer for every single one of these (and the other
series) - but a handful are unreachable due to a 2-statement read
being emitted as a single read by the compiler (which can be undone
by a targeted compiler flag). Those were included for completeness.

--

On properly - I have a follow up series that fixes the zonelist walks
structurally, but it uses SRCU-fast on the fast path. The catch is that
PowerPC (and i think others?) don't SRCU-fast without atomics, so they
take a performance hit.  (It also doesn't help us for backports)

I've been weighing that solution over never freeing and/or prepopulating
zones - but this also results in performance impacts (leaving dead zones
in the list forces the allocator to walk them for no value).

-- 
~Gregory

tl;dr:

while these are ai-generated, I'm not posting untested, unreviewed
schlopp for the sake of getting patches upstream.

I actually care about this issue.

---

Some of these don't show up as crashes but instead as hung offline
attempts (GFP_KERNEL landing on movable).  That shows up as our
hypervisor just failing to overcommit / guests hanging.

The compaction/alloc_contig bug is one I've seen in production.

I've seen a handful of NULL derefs in production i suspect are the
zonelist derefs, but it's hard to say (they're rare enough that we
lose the dumps before someone has a thought to pull them).

I expect this will scale up as we increase the number of VMs and
the amount of hotplug we're doing.

For clarity, we use ballooning + hotplug as a combo:
 - ballooning to do cooperative overcommit
 - hotplug to defend host stability when the overcommit ratio is extreme
   (basically deflate_on_oom because a host stability issue)

We're pretty aggressive about ballooning guests to squeeze them and
dropping the balloon to hot-unplug that memory when a guest is cold.


skippable side note on virtio-balloon/mem:

I have some improvements to virtio-balloon and virtio-mem that allow for
handoff from balloon directly to mem for hot-unplug without having to
drop the balloon, but I haven't posted those (and we don't run those in
production, so this is not the nexus of crashes).

Similarly I have another extension that lets the host set a "NO_ALLOC"
bit on the zone so a balloon-only system can provide the host a way to
defend against mass deflate_on_oom events (we prefer guests to OOM and
have the host to kill them due to PSI, rather than threaten host
stability). Also not in production and not posted, but there have been
a lot of experiments on overcommit here.

> The thing is that the race between offlining and actual memory removal
> is extremely long. Poisoning is just a mechanism to find these theoretical
> issues that could cause issues faster.
> 
> And with all the other things going on, this has lower priority, and I
> wanted to get back to the AI patches I have once the other thing is sorted.
> 

Sure, I realize I'm pushing this out during the merge window, but I
felt it reasonable to get some thoughts on whether these fixes are
worth considering for the window.

If the answer is no just put em on the back burner. We can carry
fixes as-needed.

> > 
> > I have a reproducer for every single one of these (and the other
> > series) - but a handful are unreachable due to a 2-statement read
> > being emitted as a single read by the compiler (which can be undone
> > by a targeted compiler flag). Those were included for completeness.
> 
> 
> In general, I think we want something that will protect all
> pfn_to_online_page() + zone->contig thingies, not just selected ones.
> We discussed in the past using some sort of rcu for that.
>
> get_online_mems is usually the wrong approach.
> 

Agreed, although the PFN walks seem genuinely harder to wrangle into an
good rcu shape.

get_online_mems seems like the backportable solution, but i can see not
wanting to have to go clean it back up later.

> protecting individual pfn_to_online_page is just whack a mole. Likely we want a
> way to check whether pfn_to_online_page() is called from the wrong context, and
> maybe we need a variant that can be called from "wrong context" for a good reason.
> 

Agreed.  Although I fear the structural fix for this will have uglier
performance issue than we think.  Then again, maybe it doesn't matter
(if you're doing a PFN walk, then you're likely already doing something
stupidly expensive).

Where i'm largely concerned is the page allocator and the
compaction/contig code not taking a regression.

For zonelists i've ended up with something like this:

@@ -1461,7 +1466,7 @@ typedef struct pglist_data {
         * Generally the first zones will be references to this node's
         * node_zones.
         */
-       struct zonelist node_zonelists[MAX_ZONELISTS];
+       struct zonelist __rcu *node_zonelists[MAX_ZONELISTS];

...

static inline struct srcu_ctr __percpu *zonelist_read_lock(void)
{
       return srcu_read_lock_fast(&zonelist_srcu);
}

static inline void zonelist_read_unlock(struct srcu_ctr __percpu *scp)
{
       srcu_read_unlock_fast(&zonelist_srcu, scp);
}

DEFINE_LOCK_GUARD_0(zonelist_read, _T->scp = zonelist_read_lock(),
                   zonelist_read_unlock(_T->scp),
                   struct srcu_ctr __percpu *scp)

static inline struct zonelist *pgdat_zonelist(pg_data_t *pgdat, int type)
{
       return zonelist_dereference(pgdat->node_zonelists[type]);
}

With this the zonelist cookie checks evaporate, but you still end up
with guard() at every walk location - not great, but better i suppose.

I was hoping this could be sunken into the zonelist walk macros, but
I haven't seen that pan out.  I've been spending a lot of time auditing
that code for private nodes as well.


On performance:

I've been hammering this with will-it-scale and a bunch of workload
tests on a variety of hosts and have not seen any obvious regressions
outside of powerpc (which i can't test directly, but SRCU-fast requires
atomics for powerpc from what i can tell - so it will eat overhead
without a fallback... if there is actually any hotplug ppc hardware out
there to care about?)

~Gregory

> There really isn't a lot on the design in the cover letter.
>

Fair, there's a mix of design vs firefighting tension going on here.

I'm trying to get out ahead of a rapidly evolving scaling issue before
it becomes big.  But I will admit it's sometimes hard to even figure
out what questions to ask without posting the wrong patches first.

(Cunningham's law :P)

> > Some of these don't show up as crashes but instead as hung offline
> > attempts (GFP_KERNEL landing on movable).  That shows up as our
> > hypervisor just failing to overcommit / guests hanging.
> > 
> > The compaction/alloc_contig bug is one I've seen in production.
> 
> Right, but I suspect that's "[PATCH 4/6] mm/page_alloc: don't undo another
> isolation in alloc_contig_range" which is *not at all* related to "mm: fix pfn
> walker races with memory offline".
>

Ehhh... i'd argue it's the same shape.  But i'll concede some of these
could be pulled out ahead as you've said elsewhere.

> > skippable side note on virtio-balloon/mem:
> > 
> > I have some improvements to virtio-balloon and virtio-mem that allow for
> > handoff from balloon directly to mem for hot-unplug without having to
> > drop the balloon, but I haven't posted those (and we don't run those in
> > production, so this is not the nexus of crashes).
> 
> Do you mean free-page reporting or actual balloon inflation/deflation paired
> with virtio-mem. I hope not the latter, because that is just disgusting.
> 

Actual balloon inflation/deflation.  It is disgusting, but it's
effective.  Right now we use hotplug at boot to minimize the size of
cold machines to avoid thundering herd issues, and then add it when
they actually get allocated for work.

At runtime we use ballooning to reduce a VM to its actual RSS based
on PSI feedback (see: that virtio stats extension i sent you some while
ago), but would like to use hotplug to give the host a defense against
a storm of deflate_on_oom scenarios across a large number of guests.

Basically using PSI stats we can make guests do somewhat aggressive
proactive reclaim on without having to build any new tooling, and
while being able to back-off aggressively.

Free-page reporting is also on by default, but this only helps you if
your guests are being extra nice about things.

(spoiler: they're not! they're greedly little imps! :D)

My experiments made balloon prefer to try to reclaim movable regions
in contiguous blocks, and then just hand that contiguous block off to
virtio-mem for offline without having to drop the balloon.

tl;dr: This seems effective and works quite well.

I've become a little confused as to why virtio balloon and mem are
separate entities when they could work together this way.

Haven't had the time to read up on the development history.

> > 
> > Agreed.  Although I fear the structural fix for this will have uglier
> > performance issue than we think.  Then again, maybe it doesn't matter
> > (if you're doing a PFN walk, then you're likely already doing something
> > stupidly expensive).
> 
> Which areas are you mostly concerned about and from which piece do you expect
> the overhead?
> 

largely the zonelist iteration - which looking back through history 
has already been "optimized" multiple times, only for those things
to introduce more bugs :\.

The zonelist races seem more ammenable to RCU solutions than the pfn
walks.  I'm not sure how to safely quiesce every possible PFN walker
without atomics in their fast path.

> For "broader" things that are not just a simple pfn_to_online_page() user, we
> could also have memory online/offline sync in more efficient ways using the
> memory notifier.
> 
> It's also tricky, but maybe for zone->contig + compaction the better solution?
> 

This seems reasonable, I was trying not to schlopp you with a half-baked
architectural fix and instead use the available tools.

But maybe we should chat (after the window).

I'll try to be on IRC more often.

> > On performance:
> > 
> > I've been hammering this with will-it-scale and a bunch of workload
> > tests on a variety of hosts and have not seen any obvious regressions
> > outside of powerpc (which i can't test directly, but SRCU-fast requires
> > atomics for powerpc from what i can tell - so it will eat overhead
> > without a fallback... if there is actually any hotplug ppc hardware out
> > there to care about?)
> 
> So the big problem is the overhead on the read side for srcu?
>

Yes - but specifically this affects PowerPC because it:
 - has NMI
 - does NOT have NMI-safe per-CPU operations
 - supports memory hotplug
 - uses atomics for SRCU-fast

So using SRCU fast in these contexts is not cheap.

For what I could measure in QEMU, the atomics on PPC seem like they
could be significant (15-20% overhead) in microbenchmarks.

Hard to say what that turns into on real hardware.

I implemented a fallback solution for those architectures to just not
free the zonelists in this case.  They have to iterate the dead zones,
but it's a much less overhead comparatively (<1%, but measurable).

For everyone else SRCU-fast is imperceptible overhead.

> If we can find something else to suspend compaction, we could maybe switch to
> rcu for the remaining cases.
> 

Is long-term compaction suspension even safe? I had to make COMPACTION a
requirement for N_MEMORY_USER_NUMA in order to not generate spurious
failures due to fragmentation.

~Gregory

-- 
~Gregory

Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series adds selftests to cover the contractual behaviors of MADV_COLD and MADV_PAGEOUT, which share a page-table walker in mm/madvise.c. The tests ensure that these hints behave correctly for various scenarios, including zero-length ranges, huge PMDs, shared memory, and file ownership. The patches also include checks for swap/eviction capabilities and skip relevant tests if they are not available. The selftests cover 24 different cases and have been run on a virtme-ng guest with three consecutive passes without any skips or warnings.

Last comment 2026-10-27 Re: [PATCH 0/6] mm: fix pfn walker races with memory offline Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a bug in the Linux kernel's compaction module where it fails to wait for pfn walkers before poisoning offlined memory maps, leading to DEBUG_VM faults. The fix involves holding an SRCU-fast read section from block check to last use of pages and making remove_pfn_range_from_zone() clear zone->contiguous and wait before poisoning. This patch also takes into account the contiguous zone skipping online checks and ensures scanner positions outlive the section.

So you found them on production kernels?

We did not have a single real report of that for probably a decade or

https://lore.kernel.org/all/20260903095543.3752338-1-yuan1.liu@intel.com/

"We are not sure whether this race is reachable under realistic

Where I motivated that we need something clean.

---

Right. And DEBUG_VM reports are irrelevant in production.

The thing is that the race between offlining and actual memory removal
is extremely long. Poisoning is just a mechanism to find these theoretical
issues that could cause issues faster.

And with all the other things going on, this has lower priority, and I
wanted to get back to the AI patches I have once the other thing is sorted.

---

In general, I think we want something that will protect all
pfn_to_online_page() + zone->contig thingies, not just selected ones.
We discussed in the past using some sort of rcu for that.

get_online_mems is usually the wrong approach.

The infrastructure for that should be added in a clean separate patch, not
mangled into a patch that fixes a DEBUG_VM splat.

This is nothing to rush in or to schlop around.

The AI patch for compaction I have here does something similar to your first
patch, with a bit less churn, and converts a handful more users.

E.g.,


+/*
+ * Nothing keeps a section online once pfn_to_online_page() has returned. A pfn
+ * walker that holds no reference to the page does the check and looks at the
+ * memmap under this guard: remove_pfn_range_from_zone() waits for it before the
+ * memmap is poisoned and can be freed. That wait cannot be interrupted, and
+ * whatever a walker sleeps on under the guard, memory offlining waits for as
+ * well: leave the guard before copying to user space. mem_hotplug_lock is held
+ * during the wait: never take it under the guard.
+ */
+extern struct srcu_struct memmap_srcu;
+#define memmap_walk_guard()    guard(srcu)(&memmap_srcu)


and then

Gregory (Meta) (author) ↳ replying to David (Arm) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing concerns about the stability issues caused by pfn walker races with memory offline, specifically crashes and hung attempts due to GFP_KERNEL landing on movable memory. They acknowledge that these issues are not just theoretical but have been observed in production environments, including NULL derefs suspected to be zonelist dereferences. The author expects these issues to scale up as the number of VMs and hotplug operations increase. The author acknowledges that the feedback from David about the patch being pushed during the merge window might be inconvenient, but is open to carrying the fixes as needed if they are not deemed worthy of consideration for the current window. Author acknowledged that PFN walks are harder to handle with RCU and agreed that get_online_mems is a backportable solution, but expressed concerns about having to rework it later. The author addressed David's concern that the patch may introduce performance issues due to the use of SRCU-fast on the fast path, particularly on PowerPC architectures. The author acknowledged this potential issue and has been auditing the zonelist walk macros for private nodes, but has not yet found a solution to mitigate the performance impact.
