tl;dr:

while these are ai-generated, I'm not posting untested, unreviewed
schlopp for the sake of getting patches upstream.

I actually care about this issue.

---

Some of these don't show up as crashes but instead as hung offline
attempts (GFP_KERNEL landing on movable).  That shows up as our
hypervisor just failing to overcommit / guests hanging.

The compaction/alloc_contig bug is one I've seen in production.

I've seen a handful of NULL derefs in production i suspect are the
zonelist derefs, but it's hard to say (they're rare enough that we
lose the dumps before someone has a thought to pull them).

I expect this will scale up as we increase the number of VMs and
the amount of hotplug we're doing.

For clarity, we use ballooning + hotplug as a combo:
 - ballooning to do cooperative overcommit
 - hotplug to defend host stability when the overcommit ratio is extreme
   (basically deflate_on_oom because a host stability issue)

We're pretty aggressive about ballooning guests to squeeze them and
dropping the balloon to hot-unplug that memory when a guest is cold.


skippable side note on virtio-balloon/mem:

I have some improvements to virtio-balloon and virtio-mem that allow for
handoff from balloon directly to mem for hot-unplug without having to
drop the balloon, but I haven't posted those (and we don't run those in
production, so this is not the nexus of crashes).

Similarly I have another extension that lets the host set a "NO_ALLOC"
bit on the zone so a balloon-only system can provide the host a way to
defend against mass deflate_on_oom events (we prefer guests to OOM and
have the host to kill them due to PSI, rather than threaten host
stability). Also not in production and not posted, but there have been
a lot of experiments on overcommit here.

---

Sure, I realize I'm pushing this out during the merge window, but I
felt it reasonable to get some thoughts on whether these fixes are
worth considering for the window.

If the answer is no just put em on the back burner. We can carry
fixes as-needed.

---

Agreed, although the PFN walks seem genuinely harder to wrangle into an
good rcu shape.

get_online_mems seems like the backportable solution, but i can see not
wanting to have to go clean it back up later.

Agreed.  Although I fear the structural fix for this will have uglier
performance issue than we think.  Then again, maybe it doesn't matter
(if you're doing a PFN walk, then you're likely already doing something

Where i'm largely concerned is the page allocator and the
compaction/contig code not taking a regression.

For zonelists i've ended up with something like this:

@@ -1461,7 +1466,7 @@ typedef struct pglist_data {

static inline struct srcu_ctr __percpu *zonelist_read_lock(void)
{
       return srcu_read_lock_fast(&zonelist_srcu);
}

static inline void zonelist_read_unlock(struct srcu_ctr __percpu *scp)
{
       srcu_read_unlock_fast(&zonelist_srcu, scp);
}

DEFINE_LOCK_GUARD_0(zonelist_read, _T->scp = zonelist_read_lock(),
                   zonelist_read_unlock(_T->scp),
                   struct srcu_ctr __percpu *scp)

static inline struct zonelist *pgdat_zonelist(pg_data_t *pgdat, int type)
{
       return zonelist_dereference(pgdat->node_zonelists[type]);
}

With this the zonelist cookie checks evaporate, but you still end up
with guard() at every walk location - not great, but better i suppose.

I was hoping this could be sunken into the zonelist walk macros, but
I haven't seen that pan out.  I've been spending a lot of time auditing
that code for private nodes as well.


On performance:

I've been hammering this with will-it-scale and a bunch of workload
tests on a variety of hosts and have not seen any obvious regressions
outside of powerpc (which i can't test directly, but SRCU-fast requires
atomics for powerpc from what i can tell - so it will eat overhead
without a fallback... if there is actually any hotplug ppc hardware out

Actual balloon inflation/deflation.  It is disgusting, but it's
effective.  Right now we use hotplug at boot to minimize the size of
cold machines to avoid thundering herd issues, and then add it when
they actually get allocated for work.

At runtime we use ballooning to reduce a VM to its actual RSS based
on PSI feedback (see: that virtio stats extension i sent you some while
ago), but would like to use hotplug to give the host a defense against
a storm of deflate_on_oom scenarios across a large number of guests.

Basically using PSI stats we can make guests do somewhat aggressive
proactive reclaim on without having to build any new tooling, and
while being able to back-off aggressively.

Free-page reporting is also on by default, but this only helps you if
your guests are being extra nice about things.

(spoiler: they're not! they're greedly little imps! :D)

My experiments made balloon prefer to try to reclaim movable regions
in contiguous blocks, and then just hand that contiguous block off to
virtio-mem for offline without having to drop the balloon.

tl;dr: This seems effective and works quite well.

Yes - but specifically this affects PowerPC because it:

 - does NOT have NMI-safe per-CPU operations

but it's a much less overhead comparatively (<1%, but measurable).

For everyone else SRCU-fast is imperceptible overhead.

Last comment 2026-10-27 Re: [PATCH 0/6] mm: fix pfn walker races with memory offline Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a bug in the Linux kernel's compaction module where it fails to wait for pfn walkers before poisoning offlined memory maps, leading to DEBUG_VM faults. The fix involves holding an SRCU-fast read section from block check to last use of pages and making remove_pfn_range_from_zone() clear zone->contiguous and wait before poisoning. This patch also takes into account the contiguous zone skipping online checks and ensures scanner positions outlive the section.

Last comment 2026-10-27 Re: [PATCH 04/46] mm/memfd: make f_pos accesses atomic Reviewed-by Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a concurrency issue in the memfd file system where multiple processes can access and modify the same file position simultaneously, leading to potential data corruption. To resolve this, the patch introduces atomic accesses for f_pos, ensuring that concurrent reads or writes on shared memfds do not overwrite each other's records. The patch also includes self-tests using the kselftest harness to verify the correctness of the changes.

This patch series addresses various issues in the mm/shmem module, including swap entry handling, quota management, and ACL enforcement. Last comment 2026-10-27 Re: [PATCH 25/46] mm/userfaultfd: wake waiters when registration modes are removed Reviewed-by Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes an issue where the userfaultfd registration mode is not properly updated when changing from MISSING|WP to WP, leading to missing pages still waiting for userspace access. The problem arises because the current no-op check only compares the presence of specific flags and does not require exact mode matches. To fix this, the patch changes the comparison logic in userfaultfd_register_range() to ensure that the current and requested UFFD modes match exactly before skipping the update. This change is necessary to prevent writers from timing out due to missing faults.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Lorenzo (ARM) ↳ replying to Gregory Price Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM

From: Gregory Price <gourry@gourry.net>

Subject: [PATCH] mm/userfaultfd: compare exact registration modes before
 skipping

Changing registration from MISSING|WP to WP returns success but leaves
MISSING enabled. The no-op check sees that WP is already set and skips
the update, so later accesses to missing pages still wait for userspace.

Skip the update only when the current and requested UFFD modes match
exactly.

Last comment 2026-10-27 Re: [PATCH 0/4] mm: avoid losing atomic VMA flags Tested-by Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series addresses a bug where atomic VMA flags are lost due to races between operations that snapshot VMA flags and the setting of VM_MAYBE_GUARD atomically under the VMA read lock. The patches add tests to verify this issue, which is fixed by ensuring that VMA flags are not dropped when set atomically. The series includes four patches: three core patches and a test patch. The test patch adds a selftest to demonstrate the bug and its fix.

[PATCH nfs-utils v2 0/4] exports: add support for new read_io= and write_io= export options 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] exportfs/mountd/exportd: track the svc_export policy highest attribute
  • [2/4] exports: Add read_io= and write_io= export options
  • [3/4] exportfs: Display and document read_io= and write_io=
  • [4/4] nfsd-iomode-advisor: recommend read_io= and write_io= for an export
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series adds support for two new export options, read_io= and write_io=, to the NFS exports system. These options allow administrators to specify the I/O mode for an export, which can improve performance by matching the I/O characteristics of the client with those of the server. The patches also include a new script, nfsd-iomode-advisor, that watches various stats on the server and advises administrators on the optimal I/O mode to use for each export. The advisor is optional and provides a recommendation based on its analysis, rather than enforcing a specific setting.

[PATCH nfs-utils 0/4] exports: add support for new riomode= and wiomode= export options 4 patches
Show 4 individual patches
  • [1/4] exportfs/mountd/exportd: track the svc_export policy highest attribute

  • [3/4] exportfs: Display and document riomode= and wiomode=
  • [4/4] nfsd-iomode-advisor: recommend riomode= and wiomode= for an export
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patchset adds support for two new export options, riomode= and wiomode=, to the Linux NFS server. These options allow administrators to specify the I/O mode for an exported file system, which can improve performance by matching the I/O characteristics of the client with the capabilities of the server. The patch also includes a script called nfsd-iomode-advisor that provides recommendations for choosing the optimal I/O mode based on various server statistics. This is intended to be a companion patchset to another submission that adds support for tracking the service export policy highest attribute.

[PATCH RFC fstests] generic: add a test for multigrain timestamps Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch adds a test to ensure that multigrain timestamps are working correctly on various filesystems, including ext4, f2fs, btrfs, tmpfs, and xfs. The test checks that the mtime and ctime appear to change even when there are multiple writes in quick succession. This is done by writing 1 byte to a file 1000 times and then checking the timestamps after each write. The patch also includes a new executable called mgtime.c that performs this test, and updates the Makefile to include it as a target.

nfsd_io_cache_read_set() still sets nfsd_disable_splice_read whenever
the global knob is switched to dontcache or direct, so
nfsd_read_splice_ok() returns false before this per-export test is
reached. An export pinned to riomode=buffered on such a server never
gets splice; it falls through to nfsd_iter_read() and does vector
buffered reads. The same applies to the splice_ok decisions in
nfsd4_encode_read() and nfsd4_encode_read_plus_data().

Separately, on the names of the export options: "riomode" and
"wiomode" are hard to read, and "mode" might suggest "permission bits"
to an administrator. How about read_io= and write_io= instead? They
follow the underscore style of the other multi-word export options
(no_subtree_check, root_squash, security_label) and read naturally:

  /srv/data  client(rw,read_io=direct,write_io=dontcache)

The values buffered, dontcache, and direct then carry the meaning
without "cache" or "mode" in the name.

The name appears in the exports syntax, the nfsd.export/content
output, the netlink YAML spec, and the forthcoming nfs-utils series,
so it's worth settling before v2 and before the nfs-utils side gets
merged.

[PATCH] scsi: mpt3sas: Time out delayed not-responding device blocks Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch addresses a deadlock issue in the mpt3sas driver where a device is blocked by a DELAY_NOT_RESPONDING event, preventing further scan attempts from proceeding. The patch introduces a delayed work to unblock expired devices after a missing delay period plus an additional 30 seconds. This approach ensures that devices are not stuck waiting for a lock and allows the system to recover from such situations.

[PATCH] statx: add STATX_ATTR_MULTIGRAIN_TIMESTAMP Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch adds a new attribute to the statx system call, STATX_ATTR_MULTIGRAIN_TIMESTAMP, which indicates whether a file's timestamps are multigrain. This allows userland applications to determine if successive changes after a query will yield distinct timestamps. The attribute is set in vfs_getattr_nosec() when the inode has IOP_MGTIME set, and it is always advertised in attributes_mask. This patch addresses a limitation in the current statx system call where there was no way for userland to determine if a file's timestamps were multigrain.

[PATCH] statx: don't leak STATX_ATTR_CHANGE_MONOTONIC in stx_attributes_mask Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch fixes a bug in the statx system call where kernel-internal attribute STATX_ATTR_CHANGE_MONOTONIC was being leaked to userland on certain filesystems, such as NFS and Ceph. The fix involves masking out this attribute from the stx_attributes_mask field when copying attributes from the kernel's kstat structure to the user-space buffer. This ensures that sensitive kernel-internal information is not exposed to userspace.

[PATCH] tools/get_maintainer.pl: accept and ignore --nogit-chief-penguins Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch updates the `get_maintainer.pl` tool to accept and ignore the `--nogit-chief-penguins` option, which was previously being passed as an argument but not handled by the script.

Signals: suggested change, configuration option
Chuck Lever ↳ replying to Roland Mainz Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Reviewer Chuck Lever questioned the need for randomizing clientids, session IDs, and COPY_NOTIFY tokens, citing that if a client or middlebox is interpreting these values, it's behavior not supported by the protocol specification (RFC 8881). He suggested there may be alternative ways to provide operational needs, such as correlating packet captures with server-side events or diffing traces between runs.
Show original comment 
I'd like to understand the use case better. Can you say concretely what
consumes the contents of these tokens, and what breaks when they are
random?

The protocol mandates that nothing on the client side should care about
the non-sequence ID parts of these cookies. RFC 8881 defines the client
ID as a server-chosen shorthand (Section 2.4), gives the stateid's
"other" field no meaning beyond identifying a set of locks (Section
8.2.2), and declares sessionid4 as an opaque array. The seqid field is
the one part of a stateid that carries client-visible semantics, and
this series leaves that field alone; it is still a monotonically
increasing integer.

So if a client or a middlebox is interpreting these values, that is
behavior the spec does not support, and I'm not inclined to add a knob
to accommodate it. On the other hand, if the need is operational, say
correlating packet captures with server-side events or diffing traces
between runs, there may be a better way to provide that than
predictable tokens.


-- 
Chuck Lever (Come to NFS bake-a-thon! https://nfsv4bat.org)

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-23 Last comment 2026-10-26 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v3 0/2] memcg: reduce cacheline contention from lruvec stat 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] memcg: reduce cacheline contention between lruvec stat updates and LRU lists Neutral Submitted 2026-10-23Last comment 2026-10-26 Michal Hocko
  • [2/2] memcg: don't go through the lruvec for folio and kmem lruvec stat updates Neutral Submitted 2026-10-23Last comment 2026-10-26 Michal Hocko
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch reduces cacheline contention between lruvec stat updates and LRU lists by rearranging the layout of struct mem_cgroup_per_node to pair read-mostly lines with cold lines, and modifying folio and kmem stat updates to avoid going through the lruvec. This change improves performance on systems that use spatial prefetching, such as Intel CPUs. The patch includes two changes: one that reorders the fields in struct mem_cgroup_per_node, and another that modifies the stat update functions to skip using the lruvec. The results show a significant improvement in pages per second for threads faulting in and freeing memory in a single memcg.

Show original comment 
Thanks.  A local AI review pointed out a thing:

  One limitation worth mentioning in the [1/2] changelog: the slab
  uses SLAB_HWCACHE_ALIGN, which need not align each object to a
  128-byte boundary.  If an object starts 64 bytes into such a pair,
  its first line can still be paired with the end of the preceding
  object rather than its own cold line.  This isn't a correctness
  issue, and the measured Skylake improvement may well be real, but the
  layout doesn't guarantee that every read-mostly line pairs with a
  cold line.

Last comment 2026-10-29 [PATCH v2] mm: move LRU size stat updates out of lru_lock in folio batches Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch moves the updates of LRU size statistics out of the lru_lock critical section in folio batches, which reduces contention and improves throughput. The stats are accumulated while holding the lock and applied after dropping it, reducing the overhead of cache misses and improving performance. This change is particularly beneficial for workloads with many threads faulting and freeing memory in a single memcg, where the hold time of lru_lock directly limits throughput. The patch also moves cost_lock into the hole after lru_lock to make room for the batch pointer, keeping struct lruvec from growing. The stats may lag behind the LRU lists by one batch, but this is within the per-cpu batching already present.

Re: [PATCH v8 3/8] fuse: {io-uring} Add CPU to queue maps Neutral LLM
Re: [PATCH v8 5/8] fuse: {io-uring} Allow reduced queues through INIT negotiation Neutral LLM
Last comment 2026-10-28 Re: [PATCH v8 5/8] fuse: {io-uring} Allow reduced queues through INIT negotiation Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch consolidates the logic for creating and updating the mapping of queues to CPU IDs in the FUSE (Filesystem in Userspace) implementation of io-uring. The current approach has two separate flows for legacy and dynamic queue paths, which are merged into a single flow. In this new flow, the mapping is updated on the first registration of a queue, and it's built from the global registered queue mask. This change also fixes an issue where requests mapped to a queue could get lost if there was a failure between adding the queue and registering its first entry. Additionally, the patch allows dynamic queues to be advertised, which enables rejecting qids that don't map to possible CPU IDs during ADD_QUEUE.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

[PATCH v2 0/2] mm: page_alloc: fix reserve access for non-blocking requests 2 patches
Show 2 individual patches
  • [1/2] mm: page_alloc: do not give all non-blocking requests reserve access
  • [2/2] mm: page_alloc: remove ALLOC_NON_BLOCK from ALLOC_RESERVES
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses issues related to non-blocking memory allocations interacting with the highatomic reserve in the Linux kernel's page allocation system. The patches fix two problems: one where non-blocking requests may take pages from the highatomic reserve, and another where they may count free highatomic pages in their watermark check. This is necessary for a subsequent patch that clears __GFP_DIRECT_RECLAIM for costly __GFP_NORETRY requests, which would otherwise lead to existing problems being widened significantly. The patches were rebased to mm-hotfixes-unstable and include changes since the previous version, including clarifications on the bug's discovery and reproduction.

Re: [PATCH] memcg: fix integer overflow in calculate_high_delay() bypassing memory.high throttle Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Re: [PATCH 2/3] mm/swap: account synchronous swap reads as memory stall Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies
Last comment 2026-10-31 Re: [PATCH 2/3] mm/swap: account synchronous swap reads as memory stall Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch modifies the swap read handling in Linux to account for synchronous swap reads as memory stalls. The change is made by adding a condition to the swap_can_merge function that checks if the working set of the current folio matches the one being merged, preventing unnecessary merging when reading from disk. This approach aims to improve performance and reduce memory stall times.

1 reviewer comment — 0 participants
0 participants
Kairui Song ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer noted that the proposed change has a trade-off between PSI accuracy and batching performance, but suggested merging the current patch with an additional fix to address the issue for sync devices, which would have limited cost due to their batch size being one folio.
Show original comment 
We are in a dilemma with this change: if it fires frequently it breaks
batching but improves PSI accuracy.  If it doesn't fire frequently, it
has no negative effect on batching and performance, and it isn't helping
with PSI accuracy either :/

But after a second thought, maybe we can live with it by having both this
posted patch merged as it is and appending your suggested fix: the two
kinds of devices end up differently.

For sync devices the batch is now always one folio, so there is nothing
fancy, everything is accurate.  Readahead batching for them only happens
on the MADV and swapoff walks, so the cost is limited.

For async, the window covers submission.  That cost grows only mildly
with the folios in the bio, block layer and dispatch work, unlike a
synchronous read.  And the previous per folio accounting was not
exact either: in a single threaded task, the throttle or cost of
a non-workingset readahead folio pays is also holding up the workingset
folio's refault, since the task cannot start the next submission until
that one returns.  Same story for delayacct.

And we are missing the completion wait either way: if a fault finds the
folio in the swap cache with its read still in flight, it blocks in
folio_lock_or_retry() in do_swap_page(), which no window covers.

So perhaps we can live with it for now by having your suggested fix
on top of this posted patch? Some comments could be helpful here and
maybe some further improvement is needed.

Last comment 2026-10-29 Re: [PATCH] mm/zswap: fix double decrement of the LRU node count on writeback Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a double decrement of the LRU node count on writeback in the zswap module by moving the accounting into the isolation callback, which simplifies the code and makes it more consistent with the normal list manipulation API. The change involves modifying several functions to use the __list_lru_del() function instead of the original list_lru_isolate() function. Kaitao Cheng ↳ replying to Johannes Weiner Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Kaitao Cheng evaluated three approaches to fix the double decrement of LRU node count on writeback in zswap. They initially considered pushing the accounting into the isolation callback, but deemed it too invasive and unlikely to be accepted. Next, they explored adding a new flag to enum lru_status to handle cases like zswap where the callback has already decremented the count, but decided against it due to its limited impact on the code. Show original comment 
I tried three approaches.

The first was "pushing the accounting into the isolation callback", as
Johannes Weiner suggested. However, as Johannes's example below also shows,
this would require extensive changes across multiple subsystems. I doubted
that such a broad change would be accepted, so I dropped this approach.

The second was to add a new flag to enum lru_status, as Yosry Ahmed suggested,
to handle cases like zswap where the callback has already decremented the
count. However, the incorrect lru->node[nid].nr_items value in zswap is only
an accounting issue: I could not find any code that reads this counter for
zswap. Changing a generic interface to fix an accounting error with no
apparent functional impact did not seem worthwhile.

The third approach is the one implemented in this patch. It keeps the changes
within zswap and fixes the accounting issue, so I think it is appropriate.

Ongoing Submitted 2026-10-21 Last comment 2026-10-26 ⏱ No recent activity [PATCH v7 0/5] mm/page_counter: move stock from mem_cgroup to page_counter 5 patches
Show 5 individual patches

  • [4/5] mm/memcontrol: move memory stock to page counters Submitted 2026-10-21
  • [5/5] mm/memcontrol: add stock to the memsw page counter Submitted 2026-10-21
Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch series moves the stock management from mem_cgroup to page_counter, allowing each page_counter to have its own per-CPU cache of pre-charged pages. This change is necessary for future work on scalable and performant page_counters, such as the tiered memcg limits series. The decoupling of memory and memsw stocks in cgroup v1 users may lead to different sizes of precharged caches, but the patch preserves the invariant that memory.usage_in_bytes is less than or equal to memory.memsw.usage_in_bytes by reporting the smaller value. This change has no functional impact on memcg v2 users.

Joshua Hahn (author) ↳ replying to Michal Hocko Neutral LLM
The author is addressing concerns about the patch's impact on cgroupv2, specifically how it will interact with new page_counters being added to struct memcg. The author acknowledges that the current implementation of stocking charge for memcg is equivalent to stocking charge for the memcg->memory page_counter and explains that their ongoing work adds new per-tier page_counters to struct memcg, which cannot use the stock fastpath due to its inability to distinguish memory residence. They argue that their patch avoids hierarchy walks for most charges. Author agrees that additional context is needed and plans to provide it by adding call traces, indicating that no fix is currently planned but more information will be provided. Author addressed a concern about the impact of separate stocking on cgroup v1 users by explaining that memory.usage_in_bytes will be reported as the smaller of raw memory and memsw values to preserve the invariant. Author acknowledged that cgroup v2 users should not see any changes in the patch series and offered to provide additional explanation on how stocking currently works. The author is addressing concerns about the behavior of reporting memory usage when memsw and memory stocks are decoupled. He acknowledges that there can be an inversion where memsw reports a higher usage than memory, but explains that this is due to how direct charging works in such scenarios. The author proposes fuzzily reporting the minimum of the two reported values, which he claims aligns with the existing fuzzy nature of usage_in_bytes.
Show original comment 
So right now for cgroupv2, there's kind of a 1-1 mapping between
page_counter and memcg. I say "kind of" because there still is a
page_counter for swap, but it takes a completely different charging
path (i.e. not try_charge_memcg).

This means that currently stocking charge for memcg is equivalent to
stocking charge for the memcg->memory page_counter.

However, one of the series that I'm currently working on [1] adds new
page_counters to struct memcg to track the usage of memory
per-memcg, per-tier. The goal is to (1) track how much memory of a
cgroup lives on which tier, and (2) to limit the usage so that a single
cgroup doesn't use up all the fast tier memory alone, and shares it
fairly with all the other cgroups on the host.

When adding a new per-tier page_counter to struct mem_cgroup that gets
charged for every memcg_charge, I cannot use the stock fastpath since
the stock does not distinguish where the memory resides. It would
essentialy boil down to this pattern:

try_charge_memcg
   \__ page_couner_try_charge(memcg->tier[0])
   \__ consume_stock()

Where every path still has to do a hierarchy charge to the tier it's
charging to, whereas this patch gets us:

try_charge_memcg
   \__ page_counter_try_charge(memcg->tier[0])
          \__ consume_stock(memcg->tier[0])
   \__ page_countertry_charge(memcg->memory)
          \__ consume_stock(memcg->memory)

So that we can avoid the hierarchy walk for most charges.

I'd be happy to submit a new cover letter which better explains this
idea!

---

Sounds good with me. I can add the call traces above which should
explain my thought process a bit more. Hopefully the problem I am
trying to solve is evident there.

---

No changes to locking. In fact this series is mostly just moving code
from mm/memcontrol.c to mm/page_counter.c, everything is essentially
the same.

---

This series should not have any changes for cgroup v2, including when
and where the cache is stocked / drained in the hierarchy. I can
include a snippet on how stocking currently works to add a visual
explanation, if that helps too.

---

Yeah, so this part is a little bit annoying. Let's just look at a single
leaf cgroup for example, where the limit for memsw and memory are both
64 pages.

charge_memcg(64)
	raw memory: 64			raw memsw: 64
	reported memory: 64		reported memsw: 64
	memory stock: 0			memory stock: 0

swap out 32 pages
	raw memory: 32			raw memsw: 64
	reported memory: 32		reported memsw: 64
	memory stock: 0			memsw stock: 0

free 32 pages
	raw memory: 0			raw memsw: 32
	reported memory: 0		reported memsw: 32
	memory stock: 0			memsw stock: 0

charge_memcg(1)
	raw memory: 1			raw memsw: 33
	reported memory: 64		reported memsw: 33
	memory stock: 63		memsw stock: 0

Where the last charge_memcg() causes the inversion. memsw tries to
charge a batch of 64 to refill the stock, but fails since 32+64=96
puts the memsw over its max limit. So it falls back to direct charging
where it charges the page_counter directly, and ends up with 0 stock
and reporting a usage of 33.

On the other hand, memory will greedily refill the stock with 64 pages
and since usage is 0, the batch refill will succeed, and it will report
a usage of 64.

So the idea with reporting the minimum is to report the minimum of the
two reported values, we will just fuzzily report the stocked value.
IIRC usage_in_bytes has always been fuzzy anyways, due to how much
memory has been stocked.

Thanks again for taking a look, Michal! I hope you have a great day : -)
Joshua

+/*
+ * HMAT and CXL report a node's bandwidth only when it first gains memory, so
+ * recompute the weights whenever N_MEMORY changes.
+ */
+static int wi_state_node_notifier(struct notifier_block *nb,
+				  unsigned long action, void *data)
+{
+	struct node_notify *nn = data;
+	int err;
+
+	if (action != NODE_ADDED_FIRST_MEMORY &&
+	    action != NODE_REMOVED_LAST_MEMORY)
+		return NOTIFY_OK;
+
+	/* The hotplug is done, failing the notification would not undo it */
+	err = wi_state_refresh();
+	if (err)
+		pr_warn_once("failed to recompute weighted interleave weights for node%d: %d\n",
+			     nn->nid, err);
+	return NOTIFY_OK;
+}

Patches Submitted (2)
Last comment 2026-10-28 [PATCH] mm/hugetlb_vmemmap: map the shared tail page writable Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a race condition between lockless page cache lookups and the allocation of hugetlb tail pages. When a page is freed and reused as a hugetlb tail page, its refcount can be read as non-zero by a lockless get, causing a write fault when trying to update the refcount with cmpxchg. The patch maps the shared tail page writable instead of restoring RCU protection, which would have added unnecessary overhead. This change ensures that stray writes to tail struct pages are prevented and all hugetlb folios of the same order in the zone remain consistent.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants

[PATCH 0/3] mm: make watermark checks honour ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT 3 patches
Show 3 individual patches
  • [1/3] mm: page_alloc: add a watermark check that knows the migratetype
  • [2/3] mm: page_alloc: make the high-order watermark check honour ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT
  • [3/3] mm: vmscan: tell direct reclaim the allocation is ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT
Neutral llm-chunked-no-replies

This patch series addresses a performance issue in the Linux kernel's memory management, specifically when using defrag_mode and 64K mTHP (machine-specific page table) faults under memory pressure. The problem arises from the high-order watermark check counting free pages of every migratetype, leading to incorrect allocation decisions. The patches modify the watermark check to consider the request's migratetype and skip free lists that cannot be used for the allocation. Additionally, direct reclaim is informed that the allocation is ALLOC_NOFRAGMENT. This change improves performance by reducing compaction stalls and 64K faults allocated under memory pressure.

Last comment 2026-10-29 Re: [PATCH 0/2] mm/cma: accept memory of CMA areas reserved with memblock_reserve() Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch allows memory reserved by memblock_reserve() to be accepted as Continuous Memory Areas (CMA) for allocation. The change aims to bring consistency in the handling of reserved memory across different kernel APIs, specifically addressing concerns about potential abuse of memblock_reserve().

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Mike Rapoport ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Inline Review Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Mike Rapoport questioned the necessity of using allocation APIs in the fixed path, citing performance concerns, and suggested that the multi-range CMA path could use allocation APIs but would be slower.
Show original comment 
Fixed path already has the range, why would it use allocation APIs?

The multi-range could, but it will be several times slower and I believe
the intention was to avoid allocation APIs exactly because of that.
View on lore ↗
Signals: performance concern, potential for slower execution
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) ↳ replying to Mike Rapoport Neutral LLM
Author responded to feedback that the patch's use of memblock_reserve() for CMA allocations is an abuse, explaining that it's actually an allocation and not a reservation. The author proposes adding a new helper function to handle this case.
Show original comment 
Having the range doesn't make it a reservation. Both paths take free
memory for the kernel's use, which is an allocation, and an allocation
has to be accepted. memblock_reserve() is for memory that is already in
use: kernel image, initrd, firmware tables. Using it for allocations
leaves acceptance to every caller, and both CMA paths missed it.

If memblock_phys_alloc_range() is the wrong tool, let's add a
memblock_*alloc*() helper that takes the exact range and does the reserve
plus memblock_prep_allocation(), the way memblock_alloc_hugetlb() finishes
its own search. There is no search, so the multi-range path doesn't get
slower, and accept_memory() stays inside memblock.

-- 
  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov
View on lore ↗
Signals: clarification, proposed fix

Show original comment 
memblock_reserve() predates any allocation APIs and it is used to say "I
know this memory is free, I'm taking it". So memory acceptance should have
take it into account.

---

A new helper that does reserve and accept makes sense, but let's do it on
top and also audit other callers of memblock_reserve(). I believe we may
also hit some curious corner cases with memmap=, although this is less
likely.

These two are really simple and easily backportable and I don't see an
issue with calling accept_memory in low level cma code.

CMA contradicts it.

Signals: APPROVAL
Last comment 2026-10-29 Re: [PATCH 42/46] mm/huge_memory: split huge PUDs when adjusting VMA boundaries Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch optimizes the truncate operation in the Linux kernel by reducing the number of lookups required when a folio is left behind in the mapping. Normally, two lookups are performed: one from the start and one from the end of the range. However, if the second loop had work to do, it means that there's still something left in the mapping, so instead of performing another lookup from the start, the patch reuses the existing loop that goes back to the start when necessary. This reduces the number of lookups required and improves performance.

4 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

(this is not hugetlb code :) )

David (Arm) Inline Review Reviewed-by Neutral LLM
Reviewer David (Arm) noted that the split_pud check is unnecessary and suggested dropping it to simplify the code.
Show original comment 
Ah, or I can just drop the split_pud check. Let me do that.

+static void split_huge_leaf(struct vm_area_struct *vma,
+               unsigned long address)
+{
+       const bool split_pud = pud_split_required(vma, address);
+       const bool split_pmd = pmd_split_required(vma, address);
+       pgd_t *pgd;
+       p4d_t *p4d;
+       pud_t *pud;
+       pmd_t *pmd;
+
+       /*
+        * mmap, VMA and rmap write locks protect the walk; the split helpers
+        * take the page table locks.
+        */
+       if (!split_pud && !split_pmd)
+               return;
+
+       pgd = pgd_offset(vma->vm_mm, address);
+       if (!pgd_present(*pgd))
+               return;
+
+       p4d = p4d_offset(pgd, address);
+       if (!p4d_present(*p4d))
+               return;
+
+       pud = pud_offset(p4d, address);
+       if (pud_leaf(*pud))
+               __split_huge_pud(vma, pud, address);
+       if (!split_pmd || !pud_present(*pud))
+               return;
+
+       pmd = pmd_offset(pud, address);
+       __split_huge_pmd(vma, pmd, address, false);
 }

-- 
Cheers,

David

Last comment 2026-10-26 Re: [PATCH 08/16] mm/shmem: stop retrying folios that cannot be split Neutral LLM

This patch series addresses various issues in the mm/shmem module, including swap entry handling, quota management, and ACL enforcement. The patches fix problems such as OOM conditions, lost quota limits, and EPERM errors when accessing sealed memory files. Additionally, the series resolves hangs caused by unkillable processes and incorrect swap count underflows. The patches were tested on an x86_64 kernel with KASAN and lockdep enabled in a virtme-ng VM, and all issues reproduced on the base kernel were fixed with the series applied.

Last comment 2026-10-28 Re: [PATCH 05/46] mm/truncate: look again if a folio left the mapping Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch addresses a potential race condition in the folio locking mechanism when truncating a file. The issue arises from the fact that the reference count check is performed before acquiring the folio lock, which can lead to unexpected behavior if another CPU has already unlocked the folio. To fix this, the patch ensures that the folio lock is acquired after the reference count check, preventing the race condition and ensuring correct truncation of files.

Last comment 2026-10-29 Re: [PATCH 05/46] mm/truncate: look again if a folio left the mapping Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch optimizes the truncate operation in the Linux kernel by reducing the number of lookups required when a folio is left behind in the mapping. Normally, two lookups are performed: one from the start and one from the end of the range. However, if the second loop had work to do, it means that there's still something left in the mapping, so instead of performing another lookup from the start, the patch reuses the existing loop that goes back to the start when necessary. This reduces the number of lookups required and improves performance.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
David (Arm) ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Needs Work LLM
Reviewer questioned the necessity of an additional lookup when the last batch reaches the end, suggesting that the code is simpler and more consistent if it exits without another look.
Show original comment 
But that's then another lookup even if not really required?

Why do you thing that is that better?

I agree that the code is simpler, but at least it's consistent with the other
handling.

-- 
Cheers,

David

Kiryl Shutsemau (author) ↳ replying to David (Arm) Neutral LLM
Author acknowledged a concern about an extra lookup in the second loop, explained that it's only necessary when the first pass didn't remove everything, and agreed to leave the choice of implementation up to the reviewer.
Show original comment 
The extra lookup only happens when the second loop had work to do.
Normally the first pass removes everything, the first lookup from start
comes back empty, and both versions stop there. When the second loop did
run to end, it costs one more lookup over a range that is now empty.

What I like about it is one way to look again instead of two: the loop
already goes back to start when the last batch ends below end, and the
patch adds a second way for when it ends at end. truncate is not like
shmem here: shmem_undo_range() stops after one sweep on hole punch on
purpose, so it needs the rewind; truncate was meant to sweep until
nothing is left.

Your version fixes the bug too, so I'll leave the choice to you.

Last comment 2026-10-27 Re: [PATCHv7 13/18] mm: Drop fake head checks Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a bug in the Linux kernel's atomic operations by allowing faults to occur when updating a read-only HVO (Hardware Virtualization Optimization) tail page, rather than attempting to handle speculative gets. The fix involves introducing a new function, arch_atomic_fetch_add_unless_nofault(), which allows faults to be handled instead of oopsing. This change is specific to x86, RISC-V, and LoongArch architectures, which support HVO. The patch has been lightly tested on x86 by hitting a real HVO tail page.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Neutral LLM
The author acknowledges a real bug in the current implementation and explains that their initial reasoning was incorrect. They propose to fix this by letting the atomic fault, which would handle the case where the struct page is a read-only HVO tail with a zero refcount. The author has lightly tested this on x86 and suggests preparing a proper patchset if the idea looks reasonable.
Show original comment 
Ughh. Yes, it is a real bug.

My reasoning in the commit message was wrong. Nothing stops a
speculative get from writing to one after the remap. On x86 even a
failing cmpxchg faults there.

The fix I have in mind is to let the atomic fault.

If the update faults, the struct page is a read-only HVO tail with a
zero refcount, so the get just fails. An exception table entry on the
cmpxchg handles it. No RCU on either side, and the fast path stays the
same lock cmpxchg loop.

Only x86, riscv and loongarch have HVO. Below is x86 only, to show the
idea; riscv and loongarch need the same primitive in their LL/SC
fetch_add_unless().

Lightly tested on x86 by hitting a real HVO tail page.

Does it look sane?

I can prepare a proper patchset if looks reasonable.

diff --git a/arch/x86/include/asm/atomic.h b/arch/x86/include/asm/atomic.h
index 75743f1dfd4e..5252b1382ed0 100644
--- a/arch/x86/include/asm/atomic.h
+++ b/arch/x86/include/asm/atomic.h
@@ -108,6 +108,25 @@ static __always_inline bool arch_atomic_try_cmpxchg(atomic_t *v, int *old, int n
 }
 #define arch_atomic_try_cmpxchg arch_atomic_try_cmpxchg
 
+/*
+ * arch_atomic_fetch_add_unless_nofault() is arch_atomic_fetch_add_unless(),
+ * except that a fault on the access jumps to @label instead of oopsing.  It
+ * works in any context, including with IRQs disabled.
+ */
+#define arch_atomic_fetch_add_unless_nofault(_v, _a, _u, label)	\
+({									\
+	atomic_t *__v = (_v);						\
+	int __a = (_a), __u = (_u);					\
+	int __c = arch_atomic_read(__v);				\
+									\
+	do {								\
+		if (unlikely(__c == __u))				\
+			break;						\
+	} while (!__try_cmpxchg_nofault(&__v->counter, &__c,		\
+					__c + __a, label));		\
+	__c;								\
+})
+
 static __always_inline int arch_atomic_xchg(atomic_t *v, int new)
 {
 	return arch_xchg(&v->counter, new);
diff --git a/arch/x86/include/asm/cmpxchg.h b/arch/x86/include/asm/cmpxchg.h
index a88b06f1c35e..685aab3edd3f 100644
--- a/arch/x86/include/asm/cmpxchg.h
+++ b/arch/x86/include/asm/cmpxchg.h
@@ -232,6 +232,63 @@ extern void __add_wrong_size(void)
 #define arch_try_cmpxchg_local(ptr, pold, new)				\
 	__try_cmpxchg_local((ptr), (pold), (new), sizeof(*(ptr)))
 
+/*
+ * __try_cmpxchg_nofault() is arch_try_cmpxchg() on a 4-byte kernel variable,
+ * except that a fault on the access jumps to @label instead of oopsing.
+ */
+#ifdef CONFIG_CC_HAS_ASM_GOTO_TIED_OUTPUT
+#define __try_cmpxchg_nofault(_ptr, _pold, _new, label)		\
+({									\
+	bool success;							\
+	__typeof__(_ptr) _old = (__typeof__(_ptr))(_pold);		\
+	__typeof__(*(_ptr)) __old = *_old;				\
+	__typeof__(*(_ptr)) __new = (_new);				\
+									\
+	if (sizeof(*(_ptr)) != 4)					\
+		__cmpxchg_wrong_size();					\
+	asm_goto_output("\n"						\
+			"1: " LOCK_PREFIX "cmpxchgl %[new], %[ptr]\n"	\
+			_ASM_EXTABLE(1b, %l[label])			\
+			: "=@ccz" (success),				\
+			  [ptr] "+m" (*(_ptr)),				\
+			  [old] "+a" (__old)				\
+			: [new] "r" (__new)				\
+			: "memory"					\
+			: label);					\
+	if (unlikely(!success))						\
+		*_old = __old;						\
+	likely(success);						\
+})
+#else
+#define __try_cmpxchg_nofault(_ptr, _pold, _new, label)		\
+({									\
+	int __err = 0;							\
+	bool success;							\
+	__typeof__(_ptr) _old = (__typeof__(_ptr))(_pold);		\
+	__typeof__(*(_ptr)) __old = *_old;				\
+	__typeof__(*(_ptr)) __new = (_new);				\
+									\
+	if (sizeof(*(_ptr)) != 4)					\
+		__cmpxchg_wrong_size();					\
+	asm volatile("\n"						\
+		     "1: " LOCK_PREFIX "cmpxchgl %[new], %[ptr]\n"	\
+		     "2:\n"						\
+		     _ASM_EXTABLE_TYPE_REG(1b, 2b, EX_TYPE_EFAULT_REG,	\
+					   %[errout])			\
+		     : "=@ccz" (success),				\
+		       [errout] "+r" (__err),				\
+		       [ptr] "+m" (*(_ptr)),				\
+		       [old] "+a" (__old)				\
+		     : [new] "r" (__new)				\
+		     : "memory");					\
+	if (unlikely(__err))						\
+		goto label;						\
+	if (unlikely(!success))						\
+		*_old = __old;						\
+	likely(success);						\
+})
+#endif
+
 /*
  * xadd() adds "inc" to "*ptr" and atomically returns the previous
  * value of "*ptr".
diff --git a/include/linux/page_ref.h b/include/linux/page_ref.h
index 9f5c75d06f76..82a2fc445ce6 100644
--- a/include/linux/page_ref.h
+++ b/include/linux/page_ref.h
@@ -246,9 +246,31 @@ static inline int folio_ref_dec_return(struct folio *folio)
 	return page_ref_dec_return(&folio->page);
 }
 
+#ifdef CONFIG_HUGETLB_PAGE_OPTIMIZE_VMEMMAP
+/*
+ * HVO maps the tail struct pages of a hugetlb folio read-only.  A speculative
+ * caller can read a non-zero refcount, then the page gets freed, becomes a
+ * tail page of a hugetlb folio and is remapped before the update.  The
+ * atomic then faults.  A read-only struct page is a hugetlb tail page with a
+ * zero refcount, so the fault means no reference can be taken.
+ */
+static __always_inline bool __page_ref_add_unless_zero(atomic_t *v, int nr)
+{
+	instrument_atomic_read_write(v, sizeof(*v));
+	return arch_atomic_fetch_add_unless_nofault(v, nr, 0, fault) != 0;
+fault:
+	return false;
+}
+#else
+static __always_inline bool __page_ref_add_unless_zero(atomic_t *v, int nr)
+{
+	return atomic_add_unless(v, nr, 0);
+}
+#endif
+
 static inline bool page_ref_add_unless_zero(struct page *page, int nr)
 {
-	bool ret = atomic_add_unless(&page->_refcount, nr, 0);
+	bool ret = __page_ref_add_unless_zero(&page->_refcount, nr);
 
 	if (page_ref_tracepoint_active(page_ref_mod_unless))
 		__page_ref_mod_unless(page, nr, ret);

Last comment 2026-10-28 Re: [PATCHv7 13/18] mm: Drop fake head checks Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a race condition in the hugetlb_vmemmap code by mapping the shared tail page writable instead of restoring RCU protection. The issue arises when a lockless get reads a non-zero refcount from a page that is then freed and reused as a hugetlb tail page, causing a write fault on read-only vmemmap. The patch also drops the write protect step for bootmem folios optimized in early boot.

15 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Needs Work LLM
The author is addressing a concern about the protection of shared tail pages in HVO (HugeTLB Vmemmap Optimization), specifically that a stray write to any tail silently corrupts all hugetlb folios of that order in the zone instead of oopsing. The author acknowledges this issue and suggests mapping the shared tail page writable as an alternative solution, which would make the race harmless.
Show original comment 
Fair enough. Here's some context.

HVO (HugeTLB Vmemmap Optimization) frees most of the memmap of a hugetlb
folio by remapping the struct pages after the first vmemmap page to a
shared page, mapped read-only.

Every lockless get of a page reference (e.g. page cache lookup, PFN
scanners) relies on page_ref_add_unless_zero(), which is
atomic_add_unless(&page->_refcount, nr, 0).

The page can be freed and reused between finding it and the get; the
zero refcount is what makes the get fail. The race:

  lockless get                        other CPU
  c = atomic_read(&page->_refcount)
  c != 0
                                      page freed
                                      page allocated as a hugetlb tail
                                      HVO remaps it read-only
  try_cmpxchg(&page->_refcount, &c, c + nr)
    -> write fault on read-only vmemmap

The refcount is zero by then and the compare fails, but LOCK CMPXCHG
writes anyway.

Before v7.1, HVO mapped the tail vmemmap pages to the head vmemmap page
of the same folio. The first struct page in each of them read as the
head, with PG_head and the head's refcount: a fake head. A get on a fake
head of a folio in use always tried the write, not only in a race, so
page_ref_add_unless() checked for fake heads before the atomic.

Back then, the race above was closed by bd225530a4c7: rcu_read_lock()
around the read and the cmpxchg, and synchronize_rcu() in HVO before the
remap. The refcounts are already zero by then, so the get either
finishes its cmpxchg before the remap or reads zero and bails.

Since v7.1, tails encode the head as a mask instead of a pointer, so
tails of the same order are identical and HVO maps them to one page of
real tails, shared by the zone. Fake heads are gone, and every read-only
struct page is a tail with a zero refcount. The race is not gone, but
32c440d67e6c and 01b1d0ffb6f7 dropped the RCU on both sides anyway, on
the wrong belief that it was only there for fake heads. That is what
David reported.

One fix is to put the RCU back. It works, but it means rcu_read_lock()
around every get that goes through page_ref_add_unless_zero(), GUP-fast
included, and synchronize_rcu() on hugetlb allocation and freeing,
including surplus allocation at fault time. So I am exploring other
options.

The fixup is one of them. With the new layout, a fault on that CMPXCHG
can only be a write to an HVO tail, so the refcount is zero and the get
fails, and the fault only happens in the window above. With GCC the loop
compiles to the same code as before; clang adds a SETE/TEST per call
site.

Writing this, I just realized that we have another option -- map the
shared tail page writable. A failing LOCK CMPXCHG writes back the value
it read, and the get never changes a zero refcount, so the race becomes
harmless. The cost is the protection: the page is shared by every
hugetlb folio of that order in the zone, so a stray write to any tail
silently corrupts all of them instead of oopsing.


And yes, I know, HVO is an ugly hack, like most of hugetlb. But it
works, and it gives back about 16MB of memmap for every 1GB hugetlb
page.

-- 
  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

Kiryl Shutsemau (author) ↳ replying to Peter Zijlstra Neutral LLM
The author is responding to a question about the state of struct pages and their refcounts after HVO remaps the vmemmap, clarifying that only the struct page itself is read-only, not the memory it points to.
Show original comment 
Emm. I am not sure I follow the question.

The memory itself is not touched. HVO remaps the vmemmap, the array of
struct pages: the part that holds the folio's tail struct pages points
to the shared page, mapped PAGE_KERNEL_RO. So it is the struct page,
and its _refcount, that is read-only.

For a 2M folio with 4K pages, its struct pages take 8 pages of vmemmap;
the last 7 of them map to that one RO page.

  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

Muchun Song ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Muchun Song expressed strong disagreement with enabling read-only (RO) only on debug kernels, citing a past experience where RO caught memory failures that led to corruption of shared tail pages under the new HVO schema. Reviewer Muchun Song expressed strong disagreement with enabling read-only (RO) only on debug kernels, citing a past experience at ByteDance where RO caught memory failures and prevented corruption of shared tail pages under the new HVO schema. reviewer pointed out that the patch does not address the issue of the tail page being remapped to RO, and suggested adding a grace period or switching the tail page to RO after some time
Show original comment 
I don't think enabling RO only on debug kernels is a good choice.

Within ByteDance, we once successfully caught memory failures using RO (because
we backported HVO, but some missing commits in memory failure caused write
operations on tail pages to persist). These operations triggered invalid writes
to shared tail pages.

Under the old HVO schema, this invalid write only corrupted one folio, but under
the new schema, it corrupts all HVOed folios. Therefore, I personally believe
that for the new schema, the value of RO is even greater.

Thanks,
Muchun

Signals: requested changes
Muchun Song ↳ replying to David (Arm) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
reviewer notes that the patch may not have caught all potential issues due to a corner case (memory failure) not being encountered during testing, and suggests additional verification is needed Reviewer Muchun Song noted that without read-only (RO) permissions on the tail page, an invalid write causes massive corruption and silently corrupts memory, making it harder to debug the crash context, but ultimately deemed this loss of debuggability as not critical and suggested restoring RW permissions to solve the race condition. Reviewer noted that the patch does not address the issue of a potential race condition when switching the tail page to RO, and suggested adding rcu_read_lock() around the get operation to prevent this
Show original comment 
Possibly, but we indeed didn't catch it during the testing phase.

The likely reason is that we hadn't anticipated issues in a corner
case like a memory failure. Also, it's worth noting that during our
test and verification phases, actual memory failures simply didn't
occur.

---

Under the new schema, an invalid write causes massive corruption,
which would likely result in an immediate and noticeable failure
(like a crash or oops). From a developer's perspective, this
definitely helps us "fail fast" and find the bug quickly.

So, let me think about what we actually lose without RO.

I believe the main difference is the debuggability of the crash context.
With RO, the hardware intercepts the write immediately and throws
a clear page fault with a call trace. Without RO, an invalid write
silently corrupts memory, and we might only find out later with no
clue where it happened.

Of course, losing this debuggability doesn't seem like a critical
issue after all. Thinking about it this way, restoring RW permissions
to fix this race condition seems like an acceptable trade-off.

Therefore, I am kind of leaning towards removing the RO protection
and restoring RW permissions to solve the race.

Thanks,
Muchun

---

Signals: requested additional verification, trade-off, acceptable trade-off
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Needs Work LLM
The author acknowledged that the swapoff path needs to drop the per-vswap spinlock before calling try_to_unmap(), agreed to restructure in v2.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>
Date: Tue, 27 Oct 2026 17:23:47 +0000
Subject: [PATCH] mm/hugetlb_vmemmap: map the shared tail page writable

page_ref_add_unless_zero() reads the refcount and then updates it with
a cmpxchg.  A lockless get, such as a page cache lookup or a PFN
scanner, can read a non-zero refcount from a page that is then freed
and reused as a hugetlb tail page.  If HVO remaps its struct page to
the shared tail page in between, the cmpxchg writes to read-only
vmemmap:

    CPU0 (lockless get)               CPU1
    c = atomic_read(&page->_refcount)
                                      page freed
                                      page allocated as a hugetlb tail
                                      HVO remaps the struct page RO
    try_cmpxchg(&page->_refcount, &c, c + 1)
      -> write fault on read-only vmemmap

The refcount is zero by then and the compare fails, but LOCK CMPXCHG on
x86 writes anyway.  The kernel oopses when hugetlb pages are allocated
at runtime with HVO enabled while a lockless get races with it.

Commit bd225530a4c7 ("mm/hugetlb_vmemmap: fix race with speculative PFN
walkers") closed the window with RCU on both sides.  The RCU was removed
together with fake heads, on the wrong belief that it was only needed
for them.

Map the shared tail page writable instead of restoring the RCU.  Every
struct page on it is a tail with a zero refcount, so a late cmpxchg
fails and leaves the page unchanged.  RCU would put rcu_read_lock()
back around every get and synchronize_rcu() back into hugetlb
allocation and freeing.

The cost is the protection against stray writes to tail struct pages.
The shared page is shared by all hugetlb folios of the same order in
the zone, so such a write now corrupts all of them instead of oopsing.

Bootmem folios optimized in early boot get their tails mapped writable
and write protected later.  Drop the write protect step.

Fixes: 32c440d67e6c ("mm: drop fake head checks")
Fixes: 01b1d0ffb6f7 ("hugetlb: remove VMEMMAP_SYNCHRONIZE_RCU")
Reported-by: David Hildenbrand (Arm) <david@kernel.org>
Closes: https://lore.kernel.org/r/ebc7bde1-f52e-4da8-b16f-822d6c0a4d74@kernel.org
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
---

+shared page. The head page is also remapped to a new page. This allows the

+The shared page is mapped writable. A lockless get of a page reference can
+read a non-zero refcount before the remap and attempt its atomic update
+after it. The refcount of a tail page is zero, so the update fails without
+changing the page. Nothing else may write to the shared page: it is shared

For bootmem folios, I don't think the RO mapping should be removed,
because we are in the initialization stage of struct page.
No one should touch those pages at this point; otherwise, they could
encounter uninitialized struct page as well. This assumes no race
exists during early boot.

Since such a problem does not exist during the boot stage, why should
we give up the protection that RO provides?

Thoughts?

Muchun

Last comment 2026-10-28 Re: [PATCHv7 13/18] mm: Drop fake head checks Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a race condition in the hugetlb_vmemmap code by mapping the shared tail page writable instead of restoring RCU protection. The issue arises when a lockless get reads a non-zero refcount from a page that is then freed and reused as a hugetlb tail page, causing a write fault on read-only vmemmap. The patch also drops the write protect step for bootmem folios optimized in early boot.

would need rcu_read_lock() back around the get, and then it is the old

So what do you need that rcu crud for?

Signals: patch assumption may not hold in all cases
Muchun Song ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Muchun Song expressed strong disagreement with enabling read-only (RO) only on debug kernels, citing a past experience where RO caught memory failures that led to corruption of shared tail pages under the new HVO schema. Reviewer Muchun Song expressed strong disagreement with enabling read-only (RO) only on debug kernels, citing a past experience at ByteDance where RO caught memory failures and prevented corruption of shared tail pages under the new HVO schema. reviewer pointed out that the patch does not address the issue of the tail page being remapped to RO, and suggested adding a grace period or switching the tail page to RO after some time
Show original comment 
I don't think enabling RO only on debug kernels is a good choice.

Within ByteDance, we once successfully caught memory failures using RO (because
we backported HVO, but some missing commits in memory failure caused write
operations on tail pages to persist). These operations triggered invalid writes
to shared tail pages.

Under the old HVO schema, this invalid write only corrupted one folio, but under
the new schema, it corrupts all HVOed folios. Therefore, I personally believe
that for the new schema, the value of RO is even greater.

Thanks,
Muchun

---

View on lore ↗
Signals: requested changes
Muchun Song ↳ replying to David (Arm) Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
reviewer notes that the patch may not have caught all potential issues due to a corner case (memory failure) not being encountered during testing, and suggests additional verification is needed Reviewer Muchun Song noted that without read-only (RO) permissions on the tail page, an invalid write causes massive corruption and silently corrupts memory, making it harder to debug the crash context, but ultimately deemed this loss of debuggability as not critical and suggested restoring RW permissions to solve the race condition. Reviewer noted that the patch does not address the issue of a potential race condition when switching the tail page to RO, and suggested adding rcu_read_lock() around the get operation to prevent this
Show original comment 
Possibly, but we indeed didn't catch it during the testing phase.

The likely reason is that we hadn't anticipated issues in a corner
case like a memory failure. Also, it's worth noting that during our
test and verification phases, actual memory failures simply didn't
occur.

---

Under the new schema, an invalid write causes massive corruption,
which would likely result in an immediate and noticeable failure
(like a crash or oops). From a developer's perspective, this
definitely helps us "fail fast" and find the bug quickly.

So, let me think about what we actually lose without RO.

I believe the main difference is the debuggability of the crash context.
With RO, the hardware intercepts the write immediately and throws
a clear page fault with a call trace. Without RO, an invalid write
silently corrupts memory, and we might only find out later with no
clue where it happened.

Of course, losing this debuggability doesn't seem like a critical
issue after all. Thinking about it this way, restoring RW permissions
to fix this race condition seems like an acceptable trade-off.

Therefore, I am kind of leaning towards removing the RO protection
and restoring RW permissions to solve the race.

Thanks,
Muchun

---

Signals: requested additional verification, trade-off, acceptable trade-off
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Needs Work LLM
The author acknowledged that the swapoff path needs to drop the per-vswap spinlock before calling try_to_unmap(), agreed to restructure in v2.
Show original comment 
From: "Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta)" <kas@kernel.org>
Date: Tue, 27 Oct 2026 17:23:47 +0000
Subject: [PATCH] mm/hugetlb_vmemmap: map the shared tail page writable

page_ref_add_unless_zero() reads the refcount and then updates it with
a cmpxchg.  A lockless get, such as a page cache lookup or a PFN
scanner, can read a non-zero refcount from a page that is then freed
and reused as a hugetlb tail page.  If HVO remaps its struct page to
the shared tail page in between, the cmpxchg writes to read-only
vmemmap:

    CPU0 (lockless get)               CPU1
    c = atomic_read(&page->_refcount)
                                      page freed
                                      page allocated as a hugetlb tail
                                      HVO remaps the struct page RO
    try_cmpxchg(&page->_refcount, &c, c + 1)
      -> write fault on read-only vmemmap

The refcount is zero by then and the compare fails, but LOCK CMPXCHG on
x86 writes anyway.  The kernel oopses when hugetlb pages are allocated
at runtime with HVO enabled while a lockless get races with it.

Commit bd225530a4c7 ("mm/hugetlb_vmemmap: fix race with speculative PFN
walkers") closed the window with RCU on both sides.  The RCU was removed
together with fake heads, on the wrong belief that it was only needed
for them.

Map the shared tail page writable instead of restoring the RCU.  Every
struct page on it is a tail with a zero refcount, so a late cmpxchg
fails and leaves the page unchanged.  RCU would put rcu_read_lock()
back around every get and synchronize_rcu() back into hugetlb
allocation and freeing.

The cost is the protection against stray writes to tail struct pages.
The shared page is shared by all hugetlb folios of the same order in
the zone, so such a write now corrupts all of them instead of oopsing.

Bootmem folios optimized in early boot get their tails mapped writable
and write protected later.  Drop the write protect step.

Fixes: 32c440d67e6c ("mm: drop fake head checks")
Fixes: 01b1d0ffb6f7 ("hugetlb: remove VMEMMAP_SYNCHRONIZE_RCU")
Reported-by: David Hildenbrand (Arm) <david@kernel.org>
Closes: https://lore.kernel.org/r/ebc7bde1-f52e-4da8-b16f-822d6c0a4d74@kernel.org
Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
Signed-off-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>

Muchun Song ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Muchun Song questioned whether the described issue is specific to x86 architecture and suggested adding a more general description. Reviewer Muchun Song expressed concern about removing the read-only mapping for bootmem folios, arguing that it's still in the initialization stage and no one should be touching those pages yet, which could lead to encountering uninitialized struct page Reviewer Muchun Song noted that the patch does not address a potential issue on RISC-V architecture, where page_ref_add_unless_zero() can read a non-zero refcount from a page that is then freed and reused as a hugetlb tail page, leading to a write fault on read-only vmemmap.
Show original comment 
Is this an X86-specific issue? If not, I suggest a more generic
description.

---

For bootmem folios, I don't think the RO mapping should be removed,
because we are in the initialization stage of struct page.
No one should touch those pages at this point; otherwise, they could
encounter uninitialized struct page as well. This assumes no race
exists during early boot.

Since such a problem does not exist during the boot stage, why should
we give up the protection that RO provides?

Thoughts?

Thanks,
Muchun

---

View on lore ↗
Signals: questioning, suggestion, requested changes
Last comment 2026-10-28 Re: [PATCHv7 13/18] mm: Drop fake head checks Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch fixes a race condition in the hugetlb_vmemmap code by mapping the shared tail page writable instead of restoring RCU protection. The issue arises when a lockless get reads a non-zero refcount from a page that is then freed and reused as a hugetlb tail page, causing a write fault on read-only vmemmap. The patch also drops the write protect step for bootmem folios optimized in early boot.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants

Muchun Song ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Reviewed-by Needs Work LLM
Reviewer Muchun Song questioned whether the described issue is specific to x86 architecture and suggested adding a more general description. Reviewer Muchun Song expressed concern about removing the read-only mapping for bootmem folios, arguing that it's still in the initialization stage and no one should be touching those pages yet, which could lead to encountering uninitialized struct page Reviewer Muchun Song noted that the patch does not address a potential issue on RISC-V architecture, where page_ref_add_unless_zero() can read a non-zero refcount from a page that is then freed and reused as a hugetlb tail page, leading to a write fault on read-only vmemmap.
Show original comment 
Is this an X86-specific issue? If not, I suggest a more generic
description.

---

For bootmem folios, I don't think the RO mapping should be removed,
because we are in the initialization stage of struct page.
No one should touch those pages at this point; otherwise, they could
encounter uninitialized struct page as well. This assumes no race
exists during early boot.

Since such a problem does not exist during the boot stage, why should
we give up the protection that RO provides?

Thoughts?

Thanks,
Muchun

Signals: questioning, suggestion, requested changes
Last comment 2026-10-28 Re: [PATCHv7 13/18] mm: Drop fake head checks Neutral LLM (per-reviewer)

This patch removes fake head checks from the Linux kernel, which are only relevant for x86-64 architecture and a few others. The checks were unnecessary because architectures like riscv and loongarch use LL/SC instructions to implement atomic operations, making the checks redundant. This change simplifies the code and reduces complexity.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau (author) Needs Work LLM
Author acknowledged that the patch is only relevant for architectures with HVO (Hardware Virtualization On), specifically x86-64, riscv and loongarch, and agreed to make the commit message arch-agnostic.
Show original comment 
Only architectures with HVO matter here: x86-64, riscv and loongarch.
arm64 has had it disabled since 060a2c92d1b6.

riscv and loongarch implement atomic_fetch_add_unless() with LL/SC only,
no CAS variant, so they see the zero at the load. That leaves x86.

I will make the commit message arch-agnostic anyway.

-- 
  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

Kiryl Shutsemau (author) ↳ replying to Peter Zijlstra Neutral LLM
Author addressed Peter Zijlstra's concern about the fake head checks on arm64, explaining that dropping RO only removes permission change and not the PFN change, which requires break-before-make. Author also mentioned that James Houghton is working on a solution using an Access Flag trick.
Show original comment 
No. The problem there is the PFN change on a live vmemmap PTE, which
needs break-before-make on arm64. Dropping RO only removes the
permission change.

James Houghton is working on it with an Access Flag trick:

https://lore.kernel.org/r/20261003002123.505555-1-jthoughton@google.com

arm64 will need the writable tail too: its atomic_fetch_add_unless() is
a CAS loop.

Last comment 2026-10-28 Re: [PATCH v3] mm: page_alloc: make defrag_mode retries follow the promoted order Neutral LLM

The patch aims to modify the defrag_mode retries in page_alloc to follow a promoted order, potentially improving system performance and reducing fragmentation.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau Neutral LLM
Confirmed that defrag_mode keeps free space in unmovable blocks for allocations, reducing fragmentation over time. However, it requires more free memory for the same workload.
Signals: NEEDS_WORK
Dev Jain ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Needs Work LLM
Expressed concerns about Android's power consumption due to reclaim stalls and compaction stalls, and suggested making defrag_mode the default.
Show original comment 

On 27/10/26 4:30 pm, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> On Tue, Oct 27, 2026 at 12:42:12PM +0530, Dev Jain wrote:
>>
>>
>> On 23/10/26 8:16 pm, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:

>>>> The diff below replaces the previous one. It needs cleanup and splitting
>>>> before it goes upstream.
>>>
>>> Proper patchset:
>>>
>>> https://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/kas/linux.git page_alloc/nofrag-watermark-v1
>>>
>>
>> Thanks! I applied the 3 commits on top and the results improved a lot. Your patches
>> make sense to me.
> 
> Thanks for testing.
> 
> Can I use your Tested-by?

Sure! Do Cc me whenever you send. I'll try reviewing if I am not on vacation : )


> 
>> But still, I see more compact_stalls and a worse compaction success:fail ratio. The
>> limitation must be by definition I believe: we still won't be able to use 64K blocks
>> from non-movable pageblocks.
> 
> Right, that is by design.
> 
> defrag_mode keeps the free space in unmovable and reclaimable blocks for
> those allocations, so they don't spill into movable blocks. Your 64K
> faults never take it.
> 
> The price is that defrag_mode needs more free memory for the same
> workload. In return, the system does not fragment over time: huge page
> availability stays steady instead of decaying under load.

Right.


> 
> The benchmark runs right at the edge: it fills 99% of MemAvailable and
> then faults another 20% in 64K. defrag_mode=0 takes the 64K chunks it
> finds in non-movable blocks without compacting. defrag_mode=1 has to
> build them inside movable blocks, and with memory full there is little
> free space there. So more compaction, and more of it fails.
> 
> The 64K fault count ends up close anyway: 20.1k vs 20.7k in my runs.

Yep, the issue is that Android folks may complain - they observe increased power
consumption due to reclaim stalls and compaction stalls and would like to
minimize them.

I would really want to see defrag_mode become the default since it attacks
the major cause of fragmentation nicely, but the assumption is that compaction
is a free resource, unfortunately on smaller devices that's not the case.

Last comment 2026-10-26 Re: [PATCH] shmem: do not extend i_size when no data was copied Neutral LLM

Patch aims to prevent i_size extension when no data is copied, but raises questions about the correct location for the fix.

3 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau Neutral LLM
Raised concerns about the patch's scope and suggested that generic_perform_write() might be a better place to fix the issue.
Show original comment 
On Mon, Oct 26, 2026 at 04:48:58PM +0100, Jan Kara wrote:
> On Mon 26-10-26 15:34:08, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> > On Sun, Oct 25, 2026 at 04:46:19PM +0000, Hungyu Lin wrote:
> > > shmem_write_end() advances i_size to pos + copied even when copied is
> > > zero. A buffered write past EOF can therefore extend a tmpfs file
> > > without writing any data.
> > 
> > + willy, jack, fsdevel.
> > 
> > This doesn't seem to be tmpfs-specific, right? I see the same with ext4.
> > 
> > simple_write_end() and generic_write_end() check pos + copied the same
> > way, so filesystems that use them through generic_perform_write() are
> > affected too.
> > 
> > It makes me think maybe generic_perform_write() is the right place to
> > fix it. Hm?
> 
> Well, that would be nice. But how'd you like to do that? i_size update is
> the responsibility of ->write_end and we have to call ->write_end if copied
> == 0 so that is undoes what ->write_begin has prepared.

Hm.

A zero-length copy only moves i_size if the write starts beyond EOF.
So we can bring the prefault back just for that case, before
->write_begin():

        if (unlikely(pos > i_size_read(mapping->host)) &&
            fault_in_iov_iter_readable(i, bytes) == bytes) {
                status = -EFAULT;
                break;
        }

Overwrites and appends keep skipping the prefault. It is racy against
a concurrent munmap(), as it was before 665575cff098, but that is
userspace racing with itself.

The per-filesystem alternative is a 'copied &&' in every ->write_end.

And generic_write_end() cannot take it as is: generic_cont_expand_simple()
relies on a zero-length ->write_end() extending i_size. So we need some
replacement.

-- 
  Kiryl Shutsemau / Kirill A. Shutemov

Last comment 2026-10-26 Re: [PATCH 11/16] mm/shmem: rescan after nonuniform folio splitting Acked-by Neutral LLM

Patch is part of a series to improve shmem handling, specifically after nonuniform folio splitting.

2 reviewer comments — 0 participants
0 participants
Kiryl Shutsemau Acked-by Positive LLM
Suggested making split state after truncate_inode_partial_folio() more explicit to improve clarity.
Signals: ACK
Zi Yan ↳ replying to Kiryl Shutsemau Neutral LLM
Pointed out that Zhang Yi's truncate fix already achieves the suggested explicit split state communication.
Show original comment 
On Mon Oct 26, 2026 at 1:26 PM EDT, Kiryl Shutsemau wrote:
> On Mon, Oct 26, 2026 at 10:40:44AM +0100, David Hildenbrand (Arm) wrote:
>> The final scan in shmem_undo_range() restarts after a successful split
>> only if the original folio is no longer large. A nonuniform split can
>> leave that folio large, so the scan skips the newly created folios.
>> Hole punching then leaves pages within the hole allocated even though
>> they could be discarded.
>> 
>> For example, a fault below the hole can allocate a 2 MiB folio after
>> boundary handling. Punching a 1 MiB hole at offset 64 KiB splits the
>> folio successfully but leaves the original folio at order 4. The scan
>> continues past all 512 pages, leaving 2 MiB allocated instead of 1 MiB.
>> The hole reads as zero, but the pages and their block charges remain.
>> 
>> Restart when the original folio is still in the mapping after partial
>> truncation, identified by checking folio->mapping. If the helper
>> removed the whole folio, no rescan is needed.
>> 
>> Fixes: 7460b470a131f ("mm/truncate: use folio_split() in truncate operation")
>> Cc: stable@vger.kernel.org
>> Signed-off-by: David Hildenbrand (Arm) <david@kernel.org>
>
> Acked-by: Kiryl Shutsemau (Meta) <kas@kernel.org>
>
> Do we want split state after truncate_inode_partial_folio() to be
> communicated more explicit? ->mapping check works, but it might not be
> obvious why. Just an idea for a follow up.

Zhang Yi's truncate fix has done that. truncate_inode_partial_folio()
returns true only if the folio is split[1]. His patches are in mm-stable
but not upstreamed yet.

[1] https://git.kernel.org/pub/scm/linux/kernel/git/akpm/mm.git/tree/mm/truncate.c?h=mm-stable#n248

-- 
Best Regards,
Yan, Zi
